DECLARATION OF BRADFORD CORNELL 
I, BRADFORD CORNELL, declare as follows: 


1. Iam Professor of Finance and Director of the Security 
Pacific Research Foundation, Anderson Graduate School of 
Management, at UCLA. I received from Stanford University a 
B.S. degree in physics and philosophy in 1970, an M.S. de- 
gree in statistics in 1974, and a Ph.D. in financial economics 
in 1975. A summary of my professional qualifications and a 
list of my publications in academic journals is attached as 
Exhibit E to this declaration. 


2. I have served as an expert witness in a large number of 
cases involving the disputed valuation of securities and other 
assets. In a number of these cases, I have presented testimony, 
either orally or by declaration, on behalf of plaintiff share- 
holders challenging allegedly preferential or unfair terms of 
securities transactions. 


3. I have been retained by defendant MCA INC. 
(“MCA”) to compare the value of the preferred stock 
(“Holdings Preferred”) to be received by defendant Mr. Lew 
R. Wasserman (“Wasserman”) to the $66 in cash per share of 
common stock offered in to the Matsushita tender offer. In 


76 


undertaking this work, I have relied on factual information 
regarding the Matsushita transactions including the text of the 
Capital Contribution and Loan Agreement and other exhibits 
to the Declaration of Emest T. Kaufmann in support of plain- 
tiff's motion for summary judgment in this action, dated De- 
cember 15, 1990. 


** * 


18. In light of the huge losses that interference with the 
transaction could impose on shareholders, economic theory 
suggests that aggregate shareholder wealth would be maxi- 
mized if the transaction were consummated; and assuming 
that legal violations were established, investors could sue for 
money damages. Based on my experience testifying as an ex- 
pert witness regarding calculation of plaintiffs’ damages in a 
number of securities actions, I believe such damages could be 
calculated in a relatively straightforward manner. Sharehold- 
ers will know exactly how much capital gains tax they have 
paid as a result of receiving the tender offer price of $66. The 
extent of tax deferral or elimination that the preferred stock 
could have created can be determined by referring to standard 
actuarial calculations. While a number of additional issues 
might have to be considered, I believe that an appropriate 
measure of damages for any legal violation could be readily 
developed without any greater expenditure of judicial re- 
sources than is normally required in securities litigation. 


19. For Matsushita to issue preferred stock, instead of is- 
suing debt and paying cash for the outstanding common 
shares, would impose huge costs on Matsushita because pre- 
ferred dividends, unlike interest payments, are not tax de- 
ductible. To estimate these costs I assume, based on the Offer 
to Purchase dated November 30, 1990, that Matsushita 
planned to finance the transaction by using $1 billion of its 
internal funds and issuing $5.2 billion in debt. For simplicity, 
I assume that the average cost of this debt will be 8.75 per- 
cent, the same as the yield on the preferred stock. Because 
Matsushita intends to raise the funds by issuing commercial 
paper, this assumption is conservative. Yields on the highest 
rated commercial paper are currently below 8.5 percent. 


77 


Furthermore, Matsushita has the highest credit rating and can 


raise funds both in the United States and Japan to refinance 


the commercial paper when it matures. 


20. Assuming that the corporate tax rate is 34 
annual tax cost for Matsushita from issuing patel am pr 
rather than debt is $154,700,000. The present value of this 
— tax cost 1s computed using the after-tax cost of funds 
or horizons of 5, 8 and 15 years. As shown in Exhibit D, the 
present values are $655,660,000 for 5 years, $969.268.000 for 
8 years and $1,524,822,000 for 15 years. ——

perjury under the |

Plaintiffs for their Consolidated Amended Complaint allege 
upon information and belief, except as to those allegations 
which pertain to the named plaintiffs and counsel, as follows: 


JURISDICTION AND VENUE 


1. This is a class action which arises under Sections 
10(b), 14(d), and 14(e), and 20 of the Securities Exchange Act 
of 1934 (“Exchange Act”), 15 U.S.C. Sections 78j(b), 78n(d), 
78n(e), and Rules 10b-13 (17 CFR § 240.10b-13) and 14d-10 
(CFR, § 240.14d-10) promulgated thereunder by the Securi- 
ties and Exchange Commission. Jurisdiction is founded on 
Section 27 of the Exchange Act, 15 U.S.C. Sec.78aa. 


2. Certain of the defendants reside in this district and 
have their principal places of business here. Many of the acts 
and transactions constituting the violations of law complained 
of occurred in this district. 

THE PARTIES 


3. The plaintiffs herein were common stockholders of 
MCA and are members of the class as hereinafter defined, 
having owned shares of MCA common stock until at least 
November 30, 1990. Plaintiffs thereafter duly tendered or 
caused to be duly tendered all such shares in response to the 
Ofver to Purchase pursuant to the Tender Offer complained of 
herein. Such tender of plaintiffs’ MCA stock was not an ac- 
quiescence in any of the wrongs complained of herein. The 
other stockholders of MCA, including plaintiffs and exclud- 
ing defendant Lew Wasserman (“Wasserman”), one David 
Geffen (“Geffen”), and defendant Sidney Sheinberg 
(“Sheinberg”), are hereinafter referred to as the “other stock- 
holders”. 


4. Defendant MCA Inc. (“MCA”) was at all relevant 
times a publicly held company whose common stock was 
listed and traded on the New York Stock Exchange. MCA is 
incorporated under the laws of the State of Delaware and has 
its principal offices in Universal City, California. MCA has 
no properties or employees in Delaware and Delaware citi- 
zens owned only a nominal amount of MCA’s common stock. 
Prior to the Tender offer complained of, MCA had about 
7,800 stockholders and about 77,700,000 shares of common 
stock outstanding. MCA is a diversified leisure and entertain- 
ment company and a major provider of movies and television 
programs and theatrical films. MCA’s other interests include 
the Universal Studio’s Tour in California, recorded music, 
retail and mail order merchandising, and book publishing. 


5. Defendant Wasserman was and is the Chairman of the 
Board and Chief Executive Officer of MCA, owned about 
4,953,992 shares of MCA’s stock (6.7%), and may have been 
deemed to have had shared voting and investment power in 
about 18.2% of MCA’s stock. 


6. Defendant Sheinberg was and is Presi i 
Guatustiiieatine is ident and Chief 
rene ener and owned about 1,179,635 shares

CLASS ACTION ALLEGATIONS 


9. Plaintiffs bring this action as a “(b\(3)” c 
—— to Rule 23(b)(3) of the Federal me Bw — 
— behalf of all persons similarly situated, namely, the 
other stockholders of MCA (excluding, however, any defen- 

ts herein; any directors, officers, employee, partners, 


MCA. Members of the class are so umerous that joinder 

. * * as i 
all members is impracticable in that over 77,700,000 shares of 
the common stock of MCA were subject to the Tender Offer 


of which about 97% were purchased on December 29, 1990 in 
and pursuant to the Tender Offer. 


10. Questions of fact and law common to the members of 
the class exist and predominate over any questions affecting 
only individual members of the class. Among the questions of 
law and fact common to the class are: 


(a) whether in and pursuant to the Matsushita 
Tender Offer, the other stockholders of MCA: (i) were given 
the same right as Wasserman, Geffen, and Sheinberg to elect 
among each of the types of consideration offered in and pur- 
suant to the Tender Offer; and (ii) have been given the highest 
type of consideration paid to any security holder in and pursu- 
ant to the Tender Offer. 


(b) whether the purchase of, and the arrangement 
by the Matsushita companies to purchase, the shares of MCA 
common stock owned by Wasserman and Sheinberg were 
otherwise than pursuant to the Matsushita Tender Offer. 


(c) whether such Tender Offer violated SEC Rules 
10b-13 (prohibiting other purchases during Tender Offer) and 
14d-10 (the best price rule); (d) whether the Matsushita Ten- 
der Offer has damaged plaintiffs and the members of the 
class. 


11. The claim of the plaintiffs is typical of the claims of 
the class as all class members sustained injury and damages 
arising out of the defendants’ acts and transactions alleged 
herein. 

12. The plaintiffs will fairly and adequately protect the 
interests of the class and have retained counsel competent and 
experienced in class and federal securities litigation to further 
insure such protection. 

13. A class action is superior to all other available meth- 
ods for the fair and efficient adjudication of this controversy 
since the class is so numerous and its membership so wide- 
spread that joinder of all members is impractical. Further- 
more, as individual damages may be relatively small, the 


expense and burden of non-class litigation may very well 


make it impossible fi mem : 
their wrongs. ~ os bers otherwise to redress

0. The Tender Offer was subject to a condition that at

“previously acquired by Parent {Matsushita]” are to be in-

(c) Timing: The Actual Purchase of Wasserman’s 
Shares Occurred During The Tender Offer 


31. Pursuant to the Tender Offer, Matsushita Acquisition 
issued the Offer to Purchase dated November 30, 1990.

the Wasserman Arrangement and then consummate the ac- 
quisition of the Shares held by the members of the class.

Purchase that it would purchase the shares validly tendered in 
the Tender Offer by accepting tendered shares for payment.

ceptance of shares for payment. Thereafter, Matsushita 
Acquisition would “pay for” shares accepted for purchase 
(Offer to Purchase, p. 6; Tender/Merger Agreement, § 1.01):

Acquisition] will be deemed to have accepted for 
payment pursuant to the Offer, and thereby have pur- 
chased, validly tendered Shares, if, as and when the 
Purchaser gives oral or written notice to the Deposi-

40. The Wasserman Special Arrangement thus contem- 
plated an elapse of time between Matsushita’s acceptance for 
payment and its payment for Shares other than Wasserman’ s, 
that is, between acceptance for payment and consummation of 
the tender offer by payment. Pursuant to the Wasserman Spe- 
cial Arrangement, Wasserman’s Shares were to be exchanged 
tax-free during that elapse. 


SHEINBERG'S SPECIAL ARRANGEMENT 


41. Simultaneously with the execution of the Merger 
Agreement, Matsushita entered into a special Tender Agree- 
ment with Sheinberg in which Sheinberg agreed to tender his 
MCA stock pursuant to Matsushita’s Tender Offer at the os- 
tensible Tender Offer price of $66 per share. 


42. In connection with entering into the Merger Agree- 
ment and as a condition to Matsushita’s willingness to enter 
into the Merger Agreement, Sheinberg’s empioyment agree- 
ment with MCA was amended on November 26, 1990. In os- 
tensible connection with entering into such amended 
employment agreement, MCA paid Sheinberg the sum of 
$21,000,000. 


43. Though ostensibly paid for other reasons, the sum of 
$21,000,000 constituted the payment of extra consideration to 
Sheinberg for his MCA stock (at least $16 per share) that he 
tendered to Matsushita pursuant to the Sheinberg Tender 
Agreement. The amount of $21,000,000 was designed to 
compensate Sheinberg for the income taxes that he would be 
required to pay in connection with tendering his MCA stock 
to Matsushita pursuant to the Sheinberg tender offer at the 
ostensible price of $66 per share. 


44. Sheinberg’s Special Arrangement (the payment of 
$21,000,000) was designed to make the sale of Sheinberg’s 
MCA stock to Matsushita tax-free, just as Wasserman’s Spe- 
cial Arrangement made the sale of Wasserman’s MCA stock 
to Matsushita tax-free. 


92 


THE VIOLATIONS OF RULE 14d-10 


45. SEC Rule 14d-10 provides: 
“(a) No bidder shall make a tender offer unless: 


* * * 


“(2) The consideration paid to any security holder 
pursuant to the tender offer is the highest considera- 
tion paid to any other security holder during such ten- 
der offer. 

* * * 


“(c) Paragraph (a)(2) of this section shall not pro- 
hibit the offer of more than one type of consideration 
in a tender offer, Provided, That: 


(1) Security holders are afforded equal right to 
elect among each of the types of consideration of- 


fered...” 


46. In the Matsushita Tender Offer, Matsushita and Mat- 
sushita Acquisition paid the other stockholders $66 per share 
in cash for their stock in MCA. Wasserman and Sheinberg 
were treated specially, differently, and more favorably than 
the other stockholders of MCA. Unlike the other stockholders 
of MCA, other than Geffen, only Wasserman and Sheinberg 
had the right to elect to receive for his MCA stock the pre- 
ferred stock of Matsushita Holding in a tax-free exchange. 
And only Sheinberg received an additional payment (of $21 
million) to defray his tax obligations. 


47. In selling his MCA shares, Wasserman considered the 
tax-free preferred stock of Matsushita Holding to be consid- 
eration preferable to $66 in cash. If the taxable and tax-free 
consideration were truly equivalent, Wasserman would have 
been economically indifferent between them. 


48. The Matsushita companies violated SEC Rule 14d-10 
by offering and paying a type of consideration to Wasserman 


which was different from and of a higher value (tax-free pre- 
ferred stock) than the $66 in cash being offered to the other 


| 
| 


taxes. As a result, plaintiffs and 
in an amount to be determined at a — tae 


THE VIOLATIONS OF RULE 10b-13 
49. SEC Rule 10b-13 Provides in pertinent part: 


2 = ee for his MCA stock violated Rule 10b-13 
eee 8 an arrangement to purchase their

known to the holders of the security to be acquired until the 
expiration of the period, including any extensions thereof, 
during which securities tendered pursuant to such Tender Of- 
fer may be by the terms of such Tender Offer have been ac- 
cepted or rejected. 


51. The Matsushita companies’ purchase of Wasserman 's 
and Sheinberg’s stock and the Matsushita Tender Offer were 
negotiated simultaneously, occurred at the same time, and 
each was conditioned on the other. The Matsushita companies 
agreed not to accept for purchase any shares in the Matsushita 
Tender Offer until the arrangements with Wasserman and 
Sheinberg were consummated. Defendants’ violations of Rule 
10b-13 damaged plaintiffs and the class in an amount to be 
determined at trial. 


AIDING AND ABETTING 


52. Defendants herein aided and abetted each other in the 
aforesaid violations of Rule 14d-10, and Rule 10b-13. Was- 
serman, Sheinberg and MCA caused the Matsushita compa- 
nies to violate Rule 10b-13 and 14d-10 by refusing to 
participate in the Tender Offer unless the Matsushita compa- 
nies treated Wasserman and Sheinberg specially, differently, 
and more favorably than the other stockholders of MCA, 
other than Geffen, by granting them the right in, pursuant to, 
and during the Tender Offer to receive the fully secured, non- 
taxable preferred stock of Matsushita Holding in a tax-free 
exchange, and also by offering and paying an extra $21 mil- 
lion to Sheinberg to defray his taxes. 


53. As heretofore pleaded, defendants Wasserman and 
Sheinberg were also control persons of MCA with respect to 
the wrongs alleged within the meaning of Section 20 of the 
Exchange Act. 


54. After the consummation of the Tender Offer on De- 
cember 29, 1990, Matsushita acquired all of MCA on January 
3, 1991, by a second-step merger in which Matsushita Ac- 
quisition was merged into MCA with the result that MCA

FIRST AFFIRMATIVE DEFENSE 
56. The Complaint fails to allege facts sufficient to state 


any claim upon which relief can be granted against Answer- 
ing Defendants, or any of them. 


104 
SECOND AFFIRMATIVE DEFENSE 


57. There is no private right of action under the statutes 
and regulations on which Plaintiffs and the alleged class pur- 
port to bring suit. 


THIRD AFFIRMATIVE DEFENSE 


58. All privately-negotiated arrangements between An- 
swering Defendants, Wasserman and Sheinberg were fully 
disclosed at or before the commencement of the Tender Offer. 
Plaintiffs, with knowledge of these disclosures, tendered their 
shares pursuant to the Tender Offer. As a result, Plaintiffs 
have waived their right to assert each and every claim for re- 
lief contained in the Complaint. 


WHEREFORE, Answering Defendants pray for judgment 
against Plaintiffs as follows: 


1. That no class be certified and that this action be im- 
mediately dismissed; 


2. That Plaintiffs take nothing by reason of this action; 
and 


3. For such other and further relief as the Court deems 
just and proper.

Each of these parties has been served and has appeared. All 
Matsushita defendants and Minton stipulate that answers by 
Matsushita Acquisition and Matsushita Holding to the Minton 
complaint are deemed to be answers by MEI to Minton’s 
complaint. All other parties named in the pleadings and not 
identified in the ing paragraphs are now dismissed 
without prejudice to plaintiffs’ right to appeal the court’s de- 
cision regarding former defendants MCA, Wasserman and 
Sheinberg.

2. FEDERAL JURISDICTION AND VENUE ARE IN- 
VOKED UNDER Section 27 of the Securities and Exchange 
Act of 1934 (“the Exchange Act”), 15 U.S.C. § 78aa. Many of 
the acts and transactions constituting the violations of law al- 
leged in the Consolidated Amended Complaint occurred in 
this district. The facts requisite to federal jurisdiction are 


108 


admitted. With respect to Plaintiffs’ claim alleged under SEC 
Rule 10b-13, Matsushita does not admit that acts took place in 
this district, or facts requisite to jurisdiction, because no pri- 
vate right of action exists under that provision. Matsushita 
further states that pursuant to the terms of the Offer to Pur- 
chase, Matsushita Acquisition Corp. merged into MCA Inc. 
on January 3, 1991 and its existence as a separate corporate 
entity ended. 


3. THE PARTIES ESTIMATE THAT THE TRIAL WILL 
TAKE 7 trial days once a jury has been empaneled. 


4. THE TRIAL WILL BE TO a jury. (Class plaintiffs con- 
tend that two juries should be empaneled, one to consider pu- 
tative class issues; the other empaneled to consider the issues 
presented by the individual suitor, Walter Minton. Matsushita 
contends that such an arrangement is unnecessary and unjus- 
tifiable.) At least one week prior to trial date, each counsel 
shall deliver to court and opposing counsel: (a) proposed jury 
instructions as required by local rule 13.2; and (b) any special 
questions requested to be put to prospective jurors on voir 
dire. 

5. THE FOLLOWING FACTS ARE ADMITTED AND 
REQUIRE NO PROOF: 


1. This case arises out of the acquisition of MCA in late 
1990 by MEI and its affiliates, defendants herein, for cash 
consideration of approximately $6 billion, plus other consid- 
eration in the form of an ongoing equity interest in TV station 
WWOR (“Matsushita Tender Offer”). MCA securities were 
registered on a national exchange, and the Matsushita Tender 
Offer used i talities of interstate commerce, the mails 
and the facilities of a national exchange. On or about Novem- 
ber 26, 1990, MCA had approximately 7,800 shareholders. 


2 Defendants Matsushita Acquisition Corp. and Mat- 
sushita Holding Corp. are Delaware corporations formed by 
defendant MEI, a Japanese corporation, for the purpose of 
making a tender offer for the common stock of MCA. The 
Merger Agreement between MEI and MCA provided that 
Matsushita Acquisition Corp. would make a cash tender offer

7. THE FOLLOWING ISSUES OF FACT, AND , 

ERS, REMAIN TO BE LITIGATED AT THE TRIAL: 
Matsushita states that no factual issues remain for trial and 

Matsushita is entitled to summary judgment as a matter 0 

law. Subject to the positions advanced by the parties in 


113 


connection with the pending motions and subject to the dis- 
position made by the Court in connection with the in limine 


motions described in paragraph 13 of this Order, the parties 
submit that the following issues remain for trial: 


In preparing this schedule the parties present only the ulti- 
mate issues and do so without prejudice to their entitlement to 


offer whatever evidence they believe is relevant to such is- 
sues: 


1. Was the arrangement to pay preferred stock to Was- 
serman “pursuant to” the tender offer? 


2. Was the arrangement to pay $21 million to Sheinberg 
“pursuant to” the tender offer? 


3. Was the preferred stock that Wasserman received in 
exchange for his common stock paid “during the tender offer” 
for purposes of Rule 14d-10? 


4. Was the $21 million payment to Sheinberg paid 
“during the tender offer” for purposes of Rule 14d-10? 


Plaintiffs contend, but the Matsushita defendants do not 


agree, that the following additional issues of fact also remain 
to be litigated: 


1. Did Matsushita corrupt Wasserman or Sheinberg from 
their duties to obtain the maximum and highest consideration 
for all MCA shareholders? 


2. Did it cost Matsushita more to issue the preferred 
stock to Wasserman than it did to finance the cash purchases 
of all other shares, and if so, by how much? 


3. Was Matsushita unjustly enriched by its alleged viola- 
tions of law, and if so, by how much? 


4. Did defendants aid and abet each other? 


5. Did Matsushita make misrepresentations or omissions 
in violation of Section 14(e)? 


114 
115 

6. Did Wasserman receive higher consideration for his 
shares than other MCA shareholders? 


7. Did Sheinberg receive higher consideration for his 
shares than other MCA shareholders? (The Matsushita defen- 
dants contend that with respect to Plaintiffs’ additional issues 
of fact Nos. 6 and 7, whether Wasserman or Sheinberg re- 
ceived “higher consideration” becomes relevant if, and only 
if, they received higher payments which were during the ten- 
der offer period and/or pursuant to the tender offer). 


2. Whether any of the Plaintiff 
; s has standing to 
this action or has suffered any legally cognizable | — 


Plaintiffs contend, but the Matsushita defendants do not 


agree, that the followi iti ; 
to be litigated: ollowing additional issues of law also remain 


1. Did defendants violate Rule 10b-13? 


2. Did defendants violate Section 14(d) (7)? 


8. Was the arrangement to pay preferred stock to Was- 
serman part of the tender offer? 


9. Was the arrangement to pay $21 million to Sheinberg 
part of the tender offer? 


10. Did the arrangement to pay preferred stock to Was- 
serman constitute a variance of the tender offer pursuant to 


Section 14(d) (7)? 


11. Did the arrangement to pay $21 million to Sheinberg 
constitute a variance of the tender offer pursuant to Section 


14(d) (7)?

l. This is a stockholders’ class acti

who are being deprived of the opportunity to maximize the

Merger, Matsushita commenced a tender offer to MCA’s

rata distribution of the stock of one of MCA’s businesses,

3. The decision by defendants to sell the Company im- 
poses upon them fundamental fiduciary duties to disclose all 
material facts to the Company’s stockholders with complete 
candor and to seek the best possible transaction for the stock- 
holders through implementation of bidding mechanisms or the 
exploration of strategic alternatives designed to assure the 
maximization of stockholder value. MCA’s board of directors 
(named as defendants in this action) have been and are 
breaching their fiduciary duties to the stockholders of MCA 
by, inter alia, (1) failing adequately to disclose to MCA’s 
stockholders material facts necessary to make an informed 
decision about the Tender Offer and Merger, including sub- 
stantial conflicts of interest involving defendants Wasserman 
and other MCA senior management and MCA’s Board of Di- 
rectors, the future status of WWOR-TV, prior market contacts 
from other potential purchasers of MCA, and other material 
information, (2) giving preferential treatment to defendant 
Wasserman by paying him preferred stock rather than the 
consideration paid to the public stockholders, and (3) nego- 
tiating and entering the Merger agreement on a preferential 
basis with Matsushita and failing to implement the auction or 
other bidding mechanisms or market check procedures neces- 
sary to assure that the stockholders receive the highest possi- 

ble price in connection with the change in control. 


4. The Merger is designed by the Individual Defendants 
and other members of MCA senior management to entrench 
the officers and directors of MCA in the management and 
control of MCA and to advance their own personal interests at 
the expense of MCA’s public stockholders. Indeed, the 
Merger represents the culmination of a series of steps which 
the Individual Defendants have taken to deter and ultimately 
thwart any unsolicited expression of interest to acquire the 
Company which they have not initiated or approved. In

CLASS ACTION ALLEGATIONS 


16. Plaintiffs bring this action pursuant to Rule 23 of 
the 
Rules of the Court of Chancery on behalf of themselves and 
all other stockholders of MCA, or their successors in interest 
(the “Class”). Excluded from the Class are the defendants, 
members of the immediate families of the Individual 


Defendants or their representatives, and directors, officers, 
bsidiaries. controlled affiliates, and partners of any of the 
defendants. 


17. The action is properly maintainable as a class action 
for the following reasons: 


a. The Class is so numerous that joinder of all 
members is impracticable. As of January 31, 1990, MCA had 
in excess of 73,000,000 shares of common stock outstanding 
held by thousands of stockholders of record and beneficial 
owners, 

b. The members of the Class are scattered 

the United States and are so numerous as to make 
it impractical to bring all of them before this Court, 

c. There are questions of law and fact which are 
common to the Class and which predominate over questions 
affecting only individual Class members. The common ques- 
tions include, inter alia, the following: 

i) whether the Individual Defendants have 
breached their fiduciary obligations to plaintiffs and the other 
members of the Class by failing and refusing to attempt in 
good faith to maximize stockholder value in the sale of the 
Company; 

ii) whether the Individual Defendants have 
breached the fiduciary and other common law duties includ- 
ing the duty of complete candor owed by them to plaintiffs 
and the other members of the Class; and 

iii) whether defendants have subjected MCA to 
potential liability under S.E.C. Rules 144-7, 14d-10, 10b-13 
and 13e-3, promulgated under the Securities and Exchange Act 
of 1934 (the “Exchange Act”), thereby causing MCA to expend 
and waste corporate assets that could have been distributed for 
the benefit of MCA’s public stockholders; and 

iv) whether plaintiffs and the other members of 
the Class are being or will continue to be injured by the 
wrongful conduct alleged herein and, if so, what is the proper 
remedy and/or measure of damages. 


141 


18. Plaintiffs are committed to i i 
prosecuting this action and 
a — competent counsel experienced in litigation of 
nature. claims of plaintiffs are typical of the claims of 
other members of the Class and plaintiffs have the same inter- 
ests as the other Class members. 


19. A class action is superior to other avai methods 
for the fair and efficient adjudication of this action. nnd os 
unusual difficulties are likely to be encountered in the man- 
>, ape of this class action. The likelihood of individual 

ve members prosecuting separate claims is remote. 

. Defendants have acted and are about to grounds 
— applicable to the Class, thereby peat 
injunctive or corresponding declaratory reli i 
spect to the Class as a whole. oe

22. In June 1989, the Company opened its new Universal 
Florida theme park which will operate in direct competition 
with the Disney World facility in Orlando, Florida. MCA also 
scored a recent coup when it paid the equivalent of $550 mil- 
lion in preferred stock for Geffen Records, a leading member 
of the recording industry. It also owns WWOR-TYV, an inde- 
pendent New York area television station, and has a 50% 
stake in the cable USA Network. 


23. On July 19, 1990, defendant Wasserman announced 
that for both the three and six months ended June 30, 1990, 
the Company reported record second quarter and first half 
revenues and higher net income than the periods 
of 1989. Revenues for the second quarter of 1990 were 
$949,942,000 compared to $828,256,000 for 1989. Net in- 
come was $44,781,000 or $0.54 per share compared to 
$41,972,000 or $0.58 per share for 1989. The weighted aver- 
age number of common and common equivalent shares used 
in the determination of earnings per share for the three months 
ended June 30, 1990 and 1989 was 83,245,493 and 
73,142,872, respectively. The 1990 calculations of earnings 
per share include the equivalent common shares for the Com- 
pany’s Series B convertible preferred shares issued during the 
second quarter of 1990 in connection with the Geffen acqui- 
sition. 

24. Similarly, revenues for the first half of 1990 were 
$1,862,888 compared to $1,531,602 for 1989. New income 
was $76,359,000 or $0.96 per share compared to $69,313,000 
or $0.95 per share for 1989. 


25. MCA’s film entertainment group reported record 
revenues and operating incomes in the second quarter of 
1990. The music entertainment group also reported record 
revenues and operating income for the second quarter of 
1990. Broadcasting and cable reported record revenues and 
operating incomes in the second quarter of 1990 to im- 
proved results for WWOR-TV and the USA Network. Retail 
and mail order had higher revenues in 1990 due to higher re- 
tail store sales.

30. On November 26, 1990, MCA entered into an Agree- 
ment of Merger with Matsushita. Under the Merger Agree- 
ment, Acquisition on November 30, 1990 commenced the 
Tender Offer to purchase all of MCA’s common stock at a 
price of $66.00 per share in cash. On the same day, defen- 
dants filed Schedules 14D-1 and 14D-9 with the S.E.C. and 
distributed to MCA’s stockholders the Offer to Purchase. The 
Tender Offer expires on December 29, 1990. The Merger 
Agreement also calls for MCA to spin off to its stockholders, 
on a purportedly pro rata basis, its interest in the television 
station WWOR-TV. 


31. In addition, the principals to the transaction have ne- 
gotiated and entered into a series of other transactions and 
agreements, including the following: 


a The Option Agreement, which provides for 
MCA to grant to Matsushita an irrevocable option to purchase 
16,880,788 shares of MCA stock at a price of $71 per share in 
the event that (1) a third party commences a tender offer for 
50% or more of MCA’s stock, (2) a third party acquires 33% 
or more of MCA’s stock, or (3) the MCA Board withdraws or 
modifies its recommendation or approval of the Merger, 


b. The Capital Contribution Agreement, which 
provides for defendant Wasserman to contribute 4,953,927 
shares of MCA stock to Holding in exchange for Holding pre- 
ferred stock. Under this agreement, Wasserman, unlike the 
public stockholders of MCA, will receive consideration for 
his MCA stock in a tax-free transaction, 


c. The Tender Agreements, which provide for 
defendants Sheinberg and Geffen to tender their MCA stock 
into the Tender Offer; and 


d. The Distribution Agreement, which provides 
for MCA to spin off WWOR-TV to its stockholders. The 
Distribution Agreement also calls for WWOR-TV to adopt a

34. The Tender Offer and Merger are timed to take adv 
tage of a temporary downturn in the price of MCA Somenen 
stock. MCA s stock price has recently declined primarily due 
to the initial problems experienced in opening the Florida 
theme park, the dilutive impact of the Geffen acquisition, and 
temporary world economic conditions. Shortly prior to an- 
> yigp 4 of the Merger discussions in September, 1990, 

CA stock traded at approximately $34 to $35 per share. 
However, in 1989, the stock traded at prices considerably 
above the $66 Tender Offer price. The Tender Offer and 
Merger will occur at a time when the Company is poised to 
enjoy significantly increased earnings in the future, as the 


146 


operations of the Florida theme park are stabilized and the 
beneficial impact of the Geffen acquisition is realized. 


35. The intrinsic value of the Company materially exceeds 
the $66.00 per share and WWOR-TV stock value which Mat- 
sushita will pay in the Tender Offer. Given the Company's 
potential growth and profitability, the underlying strength of 
its assets, growth plans, resources and accessibility to public 
and private financing, the price to be paid to the public stock- 
holders is unfair and inadequate. 


DEFENDANTS’ BREACHES OF 
THEIR DUTY OF CANDOR 


36. Defendants have breached and continue to breach their 
duties of candor to MCA’s public stockholders by failing to 
disclose adequately material information concerning the Ten- 
der Offer and Merger. Under Section 4.06 of the Merger 
Agreement, MCA has the opportunity to review and comment 
upon Matsushita’s Offer to Purchase and Schedule 14D-1. 
Moreover, all defendants acted in concert with and/or aided 
and abetted one another in filing and disseminating Mat- 
sushita’s Offer to Purchase, Schedule 14D-1 and Schedule 
14D-9. Finally, MCA’s 14D-9 disseminated the Offer to Pur- 
chase as an exhibit thereto. Therefore, all defendants are re- 
sponsible jointly for the inadequate and misleading 
disclosures provided to MCA’s stockholders in connection 
with the Tender Offer and Merger. . 


37. Defendants have breached their duties of candor to 
MCA’s public stockholders by failing to disclose adequately 
the nature and circumstances of the substantial benefits that 
the Individual Defendants will receive in the Merger. The Of- 
fer to Purchase and Schedules 14D-1 and 14D-9 give the 
misleading impression that Sheinberg’s, Wasserman’s and 
certain of the other Individual Defendants’ lucrative employ- 
ment and bonus agreements, as described above in paragraph 
9, were imposed “as a condition to Matsushita’s willingness 
to agree to enter the Merger t....” and are not sub- 
stantially greater than they would be if not for the Merger 
(See Offer to Purchase; Schedule 14D-9 at 23-26). In fact, 


147 


Sheinberg’s, Wasserman’s and the other Individual Defen 
oy? personal benefits from the Merger were insisted upon 
ry CA management as the price of its willingness to nego- 
tiate with Matsushita and are far greater than the Individual 
Defendants could have expected to receive in the ordinary 
coum, Matsushita willingly agreed to pay, and encouraged 
payment of, such compensation because of its expectation 
that this would vastly limit the independence of senior man- 
on and the MCA Board in negotiating with Matsushita 
an thereby save the purchaser potentially hundreds of mil- 
lions or billions of dollars in acquisition costs that a full 
disinterested MCA Board would have insisted upon. The mon 
trayal of the management benefits as consistent with ordinary 
practice and as proceeding from the wishes of the purchaser to 
secure the services of the present MCA officers and directors 
is materially false and deceptive and calculated to present a 
arene m peeve Berane of special arrangements, which 
serious confli i ' 
— o one of interest that the defendants have 


38. Defendants also have breached their duties 
by failing adequately to disclose their intention Ab de 
facto control over WWOR-TV following the spinoff of that 
entity to MCA’s stockholders in the Merger. The Offer to 
we and Schedules 14D-1 and 14D-9 falsely suggests 
t WWOR-TV could be put up for sale on the basis of com- 
porns bidding following the Merger. In fact, the provision in 
a eee for a board of directors following the spinoff that 
a dominated by MCA management and the planned 
ae ementation of a shareholder rights plan, or “poison pill” 
ve both the purpose and effect of entrenching in the control 
dk pet ao lb the MCA designees, and preventing a sale of 
ney — station on the basis of effective and competitive 
39. Additionally, defendants have breached their du of 
candor to MCA’s public stockholders by failing tne | to 
disclose significant and material information about the vari- 
Ous Operating segments of MCA that has been concealed from 
the public stockholders but provided exclusively to Mat- 
sushita. The Schedule 14D-9 creates the false impression that 


148 


such information would not be “material” to MCA’s stock- 
holders. In fact, such concealed information would be of sig- 
nificance to MCA stockholders who must make an informed 
election between exchanging their shares in the Tender Offer 
or Merger and a statutory appraisal proceeding. Defendants’ 
failure to disclose both the material nature of this information 
and the information itself constitute breaches of defendants’ 


duty of candor. 


40. Defendants also have breached their duty of candor by 
failing to disclose prior market contacts between MCA and 
other potential purchasers which would indicate to MCA’s 
stockholders the level of market interest MCA could attract in 
an open and effective auction. The Offer to Purchase and 
Schedules 14D-1 and 14D-9 falsely indicate that “no party 
other than Matsushita had made or expressed any interest in 
making a proposal to acquire the Company .. . .” (See, e.g., 
Schedule 14D-9 at 40). Defendants have failed to disclose that 
interested purchasers were rebuffed by defendants when such 
potential purchaser refused to grant the Individual Defendants 
the generous personal benefits granted by Matsushita. Such 
information would materially assist MCA’s stockholders in 
assessing the fairness and adequacy of the Tender Offer and 


Merger. 


41. Finally, defendant Wasserman’s equity participation 
in Holdings following the Merger demonstrates that the par- 
ties are engaged in a form of “going private” transaction sub- 
ject to the disclosure requirements of S.E.C. Rule 13e-3, 
promulgated under the Exchange Act. However, defendants 
have not made the required filings with the S.E.C. which 
would be required to be made were they proceeding in accor- 
dance with Rule 13e-3. In particular, the Lazard investment 
banker’s “book” has not been made publicly available as 
would otherwise be the case were the parties proceeding in 
accordance with the requirements of Rule 13e-3. Material in- 
formation regarding the fairness and adequacy of the pro- 
posed transaction has thus been withheld from the investing 
public. Delaware fiduciary duties of complete candor incorpo- 
rate the requirement to make available in a “going private” 
transaction the materials required by Rule |3e-3.

59. In this regard, in order to meet the requirements of the

quent “business combination.”

The Court must consider whether to ve the pro

Rule 23(e). Plaintiffs (“Settling Plaintiffs”) in this action join 
the defendants in urging the approval of the settlement. The 
Objectors, who are not parties in this action but are the plain- 
tiffs in a pending action in Federal Court in California, object 
to that part of the settlement which would bar their federal 
claims and request the opportunity to opt-out of the Class or 
to have the federal claims excluded from the settlement. 


After reviewing the settlement, the Court finds that the 
State claims being compromised have little or no merit and 
therefore no value. The value of the settlement to the Class is 
also minimal. The claims asserted in the Federal Court, how- 
ever, have at least arguable merit and therefore have signifi- 
cant value. This Court, on balance, finds, in its business 
judgment, that it would be unfair to bar the federal claims and

I. PROCEDURAL BACKGROUND 


This action was commenced in this Court as a purported 
class action on behalf of the stockholders of MCA, Inc., a 
Delaware corporation (“MCA”), on September 26, 1990 -- 
one day after the public announcement that Matsushita Elec- 
tric Industrial Co. Ltd. (“Matsushita”) and MCA were nego- 
tiating a possible acquisition of MCA by Matsushita. The 
complaint named MCA and its directors as defendants. Al- 
though no agreement between MCA and Matsushita existed at 
that time, breaches of fiduciary duties were claimed on the 


160 


grounds that there was a failure by the directors of MCA to 
implement a market check procedure to ensure maximum 


shareholder value. 


On November 26, 1990, MCA and Matsushita announced 
that they had entered into a Merger Agreement. Pursuant to 
the Merger Agreement, a wholly-owned subsidiary of Mat- 
sushita would make a cash tender offer for all MCA outstand- 
ing shares for $66 per share, conditioned upon the tender of at 
least 50% of the outstanding shares and the spinoff of a MCA 
wholly-owned subsidiary, known as Pinelands. The spinoff 
asset was a corporation that owned a television station that 
Matsushita was prohibited by federal law from acquiring. Ac- 
cording to the Merger Agreement, 100% of Pinelands’ stock 
was to be distributed to the MCA shareholders pro rata in the 
form of a stock dividend prior to the completion of the tender 


offer. 


On December 3, 1990, an action was filed in the United 
States District Court for the Central District of California as 
Epstein v. MCA, Inc., et al., No. CV-90-6451-R, (“the Epstein 
action”). It was alleged in that suit that Matsushita’s tender 
offer involved a special deal for Lew R. Wasserman, MCA’s 
chairman and chief executive officer. The Wasserman 
Agreement, apparently entered into contemporaneously with 
the MCA Merger Agreement, provided that immediately fol- 
lowing completion of the tender offer, Mr. Wasserman would 
surrender his MCA stock in exchange for shares of preferred 
stock of the Matsushita subsidiary. The complaint further al- 
leged that the Wasserman Agreement was in violation of Sec- 
tion 14(d)(7) of the Federal Securities Exchange Act of 1934 
(“Federal Securities Act”) (15 U.S.C. §78n(d)(7)) and SEC 
Rules 14d-10 and 10b-13. The Epstein action named Mat- 
sushita as a defendant claiming alleged securities laws viola- 
tions and also named MCA and Mr. Wasserman as defendants 
because they allegedly aided and abetted Matsushita. 


On December 14, 1990, plaintiffs in this Delaware action 
filed an Amended Complaint and at their request a prelimi- 
nary injunction hearing was scheduled for December 18, 
1990. The Amended Complaint added an allegation of a 


161 


failure to disclose a substantial conflict of interest involvi 
in 
defendants Wasserman, MCA management and its daocetenn, 
based on a claim that Mr. Wasserman received preferential 
treatment. Matsushita was also named as a defendant in this 
Delaware action for the first time because it allegedly aided 
and abetted MCA in a breach of fiduciary duty by enterin 
into a special deal with Wasserman. The Amended Can 
plaint, however, did not challenge the Wasserman Agreement 
on the grounds that it constituted a breach of the Federal Se- 


— Act as had been asserted in the federal court in Cali- 


On December 18th, the parties in this Dela i 

a Stipulation and Agreement of Comesemies end 2 gan 
“ “Settlement Agreement”). The settlement provided for a 

ismissal of the Delaware action with prejudice and for the 
release of all the claims of any member of the Class arising 
out of the transaction, including claims asserted in the federal 
Epstein action. On that same day, this Court provisionally 
certified the Class pursuant to Chancery Rule 23(b)(2), and 


set 
oe on on the proposed settlement for January 30,

Il. LAW OF SETTLEMENTS 


The law of Delaware stro 
ngly favors volun settl 
bay yy issues. Nottingham Partners v. aed Del ‘Supr. . 
A.2d 1089, 1102 (1989); Polk v. Good, Del Supr., 507 


A.2d 531, 535 (1986); 
05 (19a) ( ); Rome v. Archer, Del.Supr., 197 A.2d 


162 


In the settlement of a class action, the Court’s role is to 
protect the Class members by balancing the fairness and rea- 
sonable of the proposed settlement, with the policy favoring 
settlements. Nottingham at 1102; Barkan v. Amstead Indus., 
Del.Supr., 567 A.2d 1279, 1283 (1989). The Court, in review- 
ing a settlement, should not decide the merits of the issues, 
but must consider the nature of the claims, the defenses as- 
serted, and the factual and legal circumstances, and then apply 
its own business judgment to decide if the settlement is in- 
trinsically fair. Jd; Polk, 507 A.2d at 536; Nottingham, 564 


A.2d at 1102. 


In determining if a proposed settlement should be ap- 
roved, this Court must review various factors including the 
strengths and weaknesses of the asserted claims, the value of 
the benefit conferred on the Class by the settlement, and the 
risks, expenses, and delays of litigation. Neponsit Investment 
Co. v. Abramson, Del.Supr., 405 A.2d 97 (1979). The Court 
must especially balance the value of all the claims being com- 
promised against the value of the benefit to be conferred on 
the Class by the settlement. Sullivan v. Hammer, Del. Ch., 
C.A. No. 10,823-NC, Hartnett, V.C. (Aug. 14, 1990), slip op. 
at p. 14, 1990 WL 114228 app. pending (upholding a settle- 
ment because there was adequate benefit to the class.) 


Ill. EFFECT OF THE RELEASE 


It is not disputed that this Court may enter a judgment in 
connection with the settlement of a class action that results in 
the release of both state law claims and exclusively federal 
claims if the claims arise from the same factual predicate, 
even if the state court could not dismiss or adjudicate the fed- 
eral claims. TBK Partners, Ltd. v. Western Union Corp., 675 
F.2d 456, 460 (2nd Cir. 1982); Jn re Mobile Communications 
Corporation of America, Inc., Consolidated Litigation, Del. 
Ch., C.A. No. 10,627-NC et seg., 1991 WL 1392 Allen, C. 
(Jan. 7, 1991) (approving a settlement of both state and fed- 
eral claims as fair.) As a practical matter, a so-called “global 
settlement” is often necessary if any settlement at all will oc- 
cur. See, Raskin v. Birmingham Steel Corp., Del. Ch., C.A. 


163 


No. 11,365-NC, Allen, C. (Dec. 4, 1990). sli 
» Allen, C. . 4, ), slip op. at 
WL 193326 (holding that the settlement was dy th wnat 


When a state court settlement o 
claims which arise out of the Rat bee, Resse Page . 
determined to be fair and to have met all due process 
quirements, the class members are bound by the release or the 
doctrine of issue preclusion. Class members cannot subse 
quently relitigate the claims barred by the settlement in a fed- 
sy — Nottingham Partners v. Trans-Lux Corp., 926 F.2d 
c. (Ist Cir. 1991); Abramson vy. Pennwood Investment Corp 

2 F.2d 759, 762 (2nd Cir.1968). A federal court is, how. 
ever, free to review and determine the effect and scope of the 
release on the federal claims. Nottingham Partners, supra; 
also, In re Mobile, supra; Abramson, supra. ice soa

The Settling Plaintiffs and defendants counter that both the 
Delaware action and the Epstein federal action sought equi- 
table relief at the time the complaints were filed, at the time 
the Settlement Agreement was entered into, and up until the 
time the merger was consummated. They therefore assert that 
the Class should be certified under Chancery Rule 23(b)(1) or 
(b)(2) which do not allow for any opting-out. 


In the context of a settlement of a class action, certification 
under Chancery Rule 23(b)(1) or (b)(2) is appropriate when 
the primary relief sought is equitable in nature. Nottingham, 
564 A.2d at 1094-96; In re Mobile, slip op. at 34; Steiner v. 
Sithe-Energies, L.P., Del. Ch., C.A. No. 9511-NC, 1988 WL 
36133, Hartnett, V.C. (Apr. 4, 1988) (approving a settlement 
without an opportunity to opt out.) 


When a Class is certified pursuant to Chancery Rule 
23(b)(8), however, and the Class is seeking only money dam- 
ages, constitutional due process requires that dissenting Class 
members be given the opportunity to opt-out of the Class. 
Phillips Petroleum, Co. v. Shutts, 472 U.S. 797, 105 S.Ct. 
2965, 86 L.Ed.2d 628 (1985); Schreiber v. hadson Petroleum 
Corp., Del. Ch., C.A. No. 8513-NC, Hartnett, V.C. (Oct. 29, 
1986), slip op. at 9-10, 1986 WL 12169 (appearing a settle- 
ment without an opportunity to opt out;) Raskin, slip op. at 
12-13; Inre Mobile, slip op. at 33-36. 


165 


An action, however, which consists primari

A. The Delaware Action 


The plaintiffs in the Delaware action allege that the Board 
of Directors of MCA breached a fiduciary duty of care im- 
posed upon them by Fevion and its progeny. The Amended 
Complaint added Matsusnita as a defendant for allegedly 
aiding and abetting the claimed breaches. These claims are 
based on the duties imposed on directors to ensure that share- 
holders receive maximum value when a change of corporate 
control is inevitable. Revion, Inc. v. MacAndrews & Forbes 
Holdings, Inc., Del.Supr., 506 A.2d 173, 182 (1986). The Su- 
preme Court of Delaware held in Revion that when it is inevi- 
table that a corporation is for sale, the di * duty changes 
from an attempt to preserve the corporate entity, to that of an 
auctioneer to ensure the maximum value for the benefit of the 
shareholders. /d. at 182. Directors are not, however, required 
to follow a set course of action in the face of a change in cor- 
porate control. Barkan, 567 A.2d at 1286-87. Rather, they 


may choose various courses of action, but they must be

B. Federal Securities Claims 
While the Objectors do not object to the settlement and 


of the state law claims as asserted in the sui i - 
suit fi 
Court, they do object to the barring of the federal’ secures 


violation claims that they have asserted in the federal action in 
California and which were never asserted in this Delaware 
action. The Objectors, in their federal suit in California, claim 
that the Wasserman violates SEC Rule 10b-13 and 
Section 14(d)(7) of the Federal Securities Act -- more specifi- 
cally, SEC Rule 14d-10 promulgated thereunder. 


C. SEC Rule 10b-13 Violations 


SEC Rule 10b-13 provides in pertinent part: 


No person who makes a cash tender offer . . . for any 
equity security shall purchase . . . any such security 
otherwise than pursuant to such tender offer. 


17 C.F.R. § 240.10b-13 (1969). This prohibition attaches at 
the earliest of either the announcement of the offer or the 
commencement of the offer and ends at the time the tendered 


shares must be accepted or rejected by the offerer. Jd The 
purpose of this rule is to protect shareholders who have ten- 


lower price, to be detrimental to the shareholders who ten- 
dered. SEC Release No. 8712 [1969-1970 Transfer Binder] 
Fed.Sec.L.Rep. (CCH) $77,745 (Oct. 8, 1969). 


The Objectors first claim that the Wasserman Agreement 
violates SEC Rule 10b-13 because it was entered into earlier 
on the same day that the tender offer was announced and it 
provided for the purchase of MCA common stock otherwise 
than pursuant to the tender offer. They therefore claim, in ef- 
fect, that the Wasserman Agreement was actually part of the 
tender offer. 


The Settling Plaintiffs and the defendants respond that SEC 
Rule 10b-13 does not apply to the Wasserman transaction. 
According to them, because the Wasserman Agreement was 
entered into earlier on the same day as the public announce- 
ment of the tender offer, it was entered into before the pro- 
hibition of SEC Rule 10b-13 attached. The consummation of 
the deal was to take place immediately following the 


169 


acceptance of the tendered shares. Therefore, they claim, the 


W 
Ob nsaction falls outside the scope of SEC Rule 


Settling Plaintiffs and defendants also assert that SEC 
poe — not apply to private purchases. City Nat. hey 
— om Financial Corp., 801 F.2d 714, 717 (4th Cir. 
wa). Although this is a correct assertion, the line between a 
S Hen Public purchase has not yet been clearly drawn.

D. SEC Rule 14d-10 Violations 
. . 
jectors is based on SEC Rule 144-10, promulgaed andes 


one type of consideration may be offered shareholders 
if security holders are afforded equal right to elect = 


who tendered into the offer would receive taxable 
The Objectos assert thatthe Wasserman Agreement therefor 


Violates SEC Rule 144-10 because those who tendered into 


the offer were not afforded the equal ri choose betwe 
the two types of consideutionefina 


170 


Settling Plaintiffs and defendants assert, however, that the 
plain language of SEC Rule 14d-10 bars the Objectors’ claim. 
While they concede that SEC Rule 14d-10(c) requires that 
shareholders be given the ity to elect between differ- 
ent types of consideration being offered, they assert that sub- 
division (c) only applies if subdivision (a) is violated. SEC 
Rule 14d-10(a) applies when one offeree is receiving higher 
value than other offerees. Defendants argue that subdivision 
(a) was not violated because the value of Wasserman’s pre- 
ferred stock is substantially less than the cash consideration 
offered by Matsushita in the tender offer. 


Objectors’ claim of a violation of SEC Rule 14d-10 also 
seems to have at least arguable merit. There were two types of 
consideration offered in the tender offer: one was cash and the 
other was securities. Although it is claimed that the value of 
the consideration given to Wasserman had less value than the 
cash offered to the other stockholders, the true economic 
value of the Wasserman consideration is uncertain. Therefore, 
SEC Rule 14d-10(c) may have requited that the shareholders 
should have been given the opportunity to have made an elec- 
tion as to which consideration to receive. This issue has also 
never been definitively addressed by the courts and this claim 
therefore also has at least arguable merit. 


This Court must carefully scrutinize a settlement that has 
the effect of barring claims of at least arguable merit that were 
never asserted in Delaware but were asserted in a suit else- 
where, especially if the settlement has little or no value to the 
class. Cf Selfe v. Joseph, Del. Supr., 501 A.2d 409 (1985).

by plaintiffs included, among other things, the claims that (i)

Agreement; (ii) defendants had breached their duty of making 
fair and full disclosures concerning the Merger Agreement, 
including, among other things, failing to disclose material 
facts concerning the compensation and bonus payments to 
certain individuals contemplated in the Merger Agreement, 
and that defendants allegedly intended to retain control over 
WWOR-TV following spin-off of MCA’s broadcast assets to 
shareholders; (iii) defendants breached their duty of candor by 
failing to disclose material information concerning the operat- 
ing segments of MCA that was provided to Matsushita: (iv) 
defendants breached their fiduciary duties by according 
“preferential treatment” to defendant Lew R. Wasserman in

impermissibly negotiated preferential arrangements in con- 
nection with the transaction whereby he . . . exchange[d] 
stock for stock and avoid{ed] costly capital gains taxes to 
which all other stockholders [were] subject”; and (v) defen- 
dants violated SEC Rule 13e-3 because Mr. Wasserman’s re- 
ceipt of preferred stock allegedly established that the

13e-3 of the Securities Exchange Act of 1934, and defendants 
allegedly failed to comply with the requirements of SEC Rule

H. On December 3, 1990, a putative class action complaint

der Offer period, and (ii) violated SEC Rule 14d-10 because 
the preferred stock received by Wasserman allegedly was dif- 
ferent or greater consideration than that made available to 
public shareholders in the Tender Offer. The complaint was 
subsequently amended to include a similar claim under SEC

berg, which defendants asserted was a bonus payment derived 
from the level of incentive compensation that had been ex- 
pected to be awarded to Sheinberg in the ordinary course but 
for the acquisition, allegedly constituted additional considera- 
tion for his MCA shares. The complaint sought preliminary 
and permanent injunctive relief and monetary damages. The 
Epstein Action complaint, as subsequently amended, named 
as defendants MCA, Matsushita, Matsushita Acquisition, 
Matsushita Holding, and Wasserman and Sheinberg; and

S. On April 22, 1991, this Court rendered its decision

and dismissal of the claims asserted in the Delaware Actions 
and the federal claims asserted in the Epstein Action. The 
Court stated in its Decision and Order, inter alia, that the state 
law claims proposed to be settled had “little or no merit and 
therefore no value”; that the value of the Initial Proposed Set- 
tlement was “also minimal”; and that the federal claims as- 
serted in the Epstein Action “have at least arguable merit and 
therefore significant value.” As to such federal claims, the 
Court found that the issues raised by such claims had “not yet 
been definitively addressed by the courts” and therefore the 
claims had “arguable merit.” The Court concluded “that it 
would be unfair to bar the federal claims and therefore the 


settlement cannot be approved in its present form”; and

leave of the Federal District Court; (xi) On March 3, 1992, 
upon the Matsushita defendants’ motion to supplement the 
record, the Federal District Court found that plaintiff and pur- 
ported class representative Lawrence Epstein prosecuted the 
California Federal Actions for 15 months under false pre- 
tenses because he deliberately concealed that the MCA stock 
owned by him was held in a tax-free individual retirement ac- 
count. The Federal District Court found that this fact demon- 
strated that plaintiff Epstein did not and could not have 
sustained any injury as a result of the Tender Offer, and, if 
known, would have disqualified him as a piaintiff in the Cali- 
fornia Federal Actions; and 


V. Plaintiffs in the California Federal Actions have ap- 
pealed the Federal District Court’s dismissals of their actions 
to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit. 
The Federal Plaintiffs also appealed the denials of their mo- 
tions for class certification and other rulings made by the Fed- 
eral District Court, including the contempt ruling referred to 
above; and 


W. On October 30, 1991, David and Andrew Minton, chil- 
dren of plaintiff Walter Minton, filed a putative class action 
complaint in the Federal District Court, entitled Minton, et al., 
v. MCA INC., et al., No. CV-91-5914-R (hereinafter, the 
“Second Minton Action”). The complaint in the Second Min-

14d-10 and Section 14(d)(7) of the Williams Act. The 
amended complaint alleged that the arrangements whereby 
defendant Wasserman exchanged his MCA stock for preferred 
stock in Matsushita Holding Corp. pursuant to the Capital 
Contribution Agreement (1) violated SEC Rule 10b-13 be- 
cause it allegedly represented a purchase of MCA stock by 
Matsushita during the period covered by the Rule that was not

Z. From and after this Court’s Decision and Order declin- 
ing to approve the Initial Proposed Settlement, counsel for the 
parties in the Delaware Actions continued to discuss a possi- 
ble alternative resolution of the Delaware Actions. In Febru- 
ary, 1992, following the Federal District Court’s decision to 
dismiss the remaining claim in the California Federal Actions, 
these discussions developed into negotiations in earnest re- 
garding an alternative settlement of the Delaware Actions. On 
June 18, 1992, counsel for the parties arrived at an agreement 
in principle to settle the Delaware Actions, subject to the exe- 
cution of a formal Stipulation of Settlement and Court ap- 
proval thereof; and 


AA. Based on their review and analysis of the relevant 
facts and controlling legal principles, the document and 
deposition discovery obtained in the Delaware Actions, their 
review of relevant pleadings and proceedings in the California 
Federal Actions, the Second Minton Action, and the Second 
Epstein Action, and their analysis and understanding of this 
Court’s reasoning and holdings in the Decision and Order, 
plaintiffs’ attorneys in the Delaware Actions have concluded 
that the terms and conditions of the Settlement hereinafter set 
forth are fair, reasonable and adequate, and beneficial to and 
in the best interests of plaintiffs and the proposed Settlement 
Class (as defined below). Plaintiffs have agreed to settle the 
Delaware Actions pursuant to the terms and provisions of this 
Stipulation after considering (i) the substantial monetary 
benefits that former MCA shareholders will receive pursuant 


(vi) the impact of principles of res judicata on the viability 0 
the Second Epstein Action in light of the Federal District 


ee 


+ 
- 


187 


Court’s disposition of the California Federal Actions, includ- 
ing the decision of the New York court to dismiss the Second 
Epstein Action with prejudice on res judicata grounds; and 
(vii) the fact that the present Settlement allows members of 
the Settlement Class (defined below) to exclude themselves 
from the Settlement Class should they so desire and thereby 
not be precluded by the Settlement from individually seeking 
to pursue the claims alleged in the Delaware Actions, the 
California Federal Actions, the Second Minton Action, the 
Second Epstein Action or any other claims relating to the 
events at issue in the Delaware Actions; and 


BB. Defendants vigorously deny all liability or wrongdoing 
with respect to any and all of the claims alleged in the Dela- 
ware Actions, but consider it desirable that the Delaware Ac- 
tions be settled because such settlement will (i) avoid the 
substantial expense, inconvenience and distraction of contin- 
ued litigation in the Delaware Actions, or any other action or 
proceeding relating to the matters being settled, and (ii) fi- 
nally put to rest plaintiffs’ claims and all other claims relating 
to the matters being settled. 


In light of the foregoing, the parties propose to settle the 
Delaware Actions in accordance with the terms and provi- 
sions of this Stipulation as set forth below. 


NOW, THEREFORE, IT IS STIPULATED AND 
AGREED, subject to the approval of this Court, pursuant to 
Rule 23 of the Court of Chancery Rules, by and between de- 
fendants hereto and the plaintiffs for themselves and for the 
Settlement Class (defined below), that all claims, rights and 
causes of action (state or federal, including but not limited to 
claims arising under the federal securities laws, and any rules 
or regulations promulgated thereunder, or otherwise), whether 
known or unknown that are, could have been or might in the 
future be asserted by any of the plaintiffs or any member of 
the Settlement Class, whether directly, derivatively, represen- 
tatively or in any other capacity, against any of the named de- 
fendants in the Delaware Actions (regardless of whether such 
defendant has been served or entered an appearance in any of

the actions) and their respective present and former officers, 
directors, employees, agents, attorneys, financial advisors, 
commercial bank lenders, investment bankers, representa-

administrators, successors, and assigns including without

the Tender Agreements, the Initial Proposed Settlement, this

foregoing, and including without limitation the claims as- 
serted in the California Federal Actions, the Second Minton

HEARING ORDER 


The parties to the consolidated actions (the “Actions”) 
having made application pursuant to Rule 23(e) of the Rules 
of the Court of Chancery, for an Order approving the pro- 
posed settlement of the Actions (the “Settlement”) in accor- 
dance with a Stipulation of Settlement filed with the Court 
(the “Stipulation”), which, together with the accompanying 
documents, sets forth the terms and conditions for the pro- 
posed settlement of the Actions and for the dismissal of the 
Actions with prejudice upon the terms and conditions set forth 
in the Stipulation; and the Court having read and considered 
the Stipulation and accompanying documents; and all parties 
having consented to the entry of this Order: 


NOW, THEREFORE, IT IS HEREBY ORDERED THAT: 


1. The Actions shall, for the purposes of the Settlement 
only, be maintained and proceed as a consolidated class action 
with the named plaintiffs in the Actions as class representa- 
tives, pursuant to Court of Chancery Rules 23(a) and 23(b)(3), 
on behalf of the following class (the “Settlement Class” and 
the “Class Period”): all holders of the common stock of MCA 
INC. (“MCA”), on or after September 25, 1990, through and 
including effectuation of the Merger (as defined in § D(i) of 
the Stipulation) on January 3, 1991, and their successors in 
interest, immediate or remote (excluding defendants and 
member of their immediate families). The Court determines, 
for purposes of the Settlement only, that the requirements of 
Court of Chancery Rules 23(a) and 23(b)(3) are satisfied. 


199

fore the Court on January 21, 1993, at 12:00 noon in the 
Court of Chancery, Public Building, 11th and King Streets 
Wilmington, Delaware 19801: (a) to determine whether the 
proposed Settlement of the Actions on the terms and condi- 
tions provided for » the Stipulation is fair, reasonable and 
adequate and should be approved by the Court, and whether a 
judgment as provided in paragraph 5 of the Stipulation should 
be entered thereon; and (b) to consider the application of 
plaintiffs — in the Actions for an award of fees and ex- 
penses. ourt may adjourn the Hearing, or any adi - 
ment thereof, without further mates > eames of re 
Settlement Class other than by announcement at the Hearing 
or any adjournment thereof.

clusion and the person or entity filing an invalid Request for 
Exclusion shall be a member of the Settlement Class. All per- 
sons who properly file Requests for Exclusion from the Set- 
tlement Class shall not be members of the Settlement Class

ORDER AND FINAL JUDGMENT 


On this 22 day of February, 1993 a hearing having been 
held before this Court to determine whether the terms of the 
Stipulation of Settlement dated October 22, 1992 (the 
“Stipulation”), and the terms and conditions of the settlement 
proposed in the Stipulation (the “Settlement”), and all trans- 
actions referred to therein or preparatory or incident thereto, 
are fair, reasonable and adequate for the settlement of all 
claims asserted herein; and whether judgment should be en- 
tered in these civil actions (the “Actions”) dismissing the Ac- 
tions on the merits with prejudice as against all of the 
plaintiffs and all members of the Settlement Class certified in 
this Court’s Order dated October 27, 1992; and the Court 
having considered all matters submitted to it at the hearing 
and otherwise and the Court having rendered its Memoran- 
dum Opinion dated February 12, 1993 approving the Settle- 
ment and awarding attorneys’ fees and expenses: 


NOW, THEREFORE, IT IS HEREBY ORDERED THAT: 


1. The Notice and Summary Notice (as defined in 4 4(b) of 
the Stipulation), is hereby determined to have been the best 
practicable notice under the circumstances and in full compli- 
ance with Rule 23 of the Court of Chancery and the require- 
ments of due process and it is hereby further determined that 
the plaintiffs in the Actions, as representatives of the Settle- 
ment Class, have fairly and adequately protected the interests 
of the Settlement Class and that the maintenance of this action 


205 


as a Class action meets all the requirements of Rule 23 
(b)(3) of the Court of Chancery. ule 23(a) and 


2. The Stipulation and the Settlement are approved as fair 
reasonable and adequate and in the best interesrs of the plain- 
tiffs and the Settlement Class, and the parties are directed to 
consummate the Settlement in accordance with the terms and 
provisions of the Stipulation, provided that any additional 
Requests for Exclusion received after the deadline set in para- 
graph 6 of the Hearing Order dated October 7, 1992 but prior 
to February 12, 1993 will be effective. 


3. (a) The Actions are hereby dismissed with prejudice on 
the merits as to all defendants in the Actions (regardless of 
whether any such defendant has been served or entered an ap- 
pearance in any of the Actions) and their respective present 
and former officers, directors, employees, agents, attorneys, 
financial advisors, commercial bank lenders, investment 
bankers, representatives, trustees, affiliates, associates, par- 
ents, subsidiaries, general and limited partnerships, heirs, ex- 
ecutors, administrators, successors and assigns (including 
Lazard Freres & Co. (financial advisor to MCA), Allen & Co. 
Inc. (financial advisor to Matsushita) and Creative Artists 
Agency Inc. (advisor to Matsushita)) (collectively, the 

Released Persons”), as against plaintiffs and all members of 
the Settlement Class (other than those who have validly re- 
quested exclusion therefrom) and their successors and assigns, 
whether known or unknown, whether directly or in any other 
capacity, without costs except as provided in the Stipulation, 
such dismissal to be subject only to compliance by the parties 
with the terms and conditions of the Stipulation and any Or- 
der of this Court with reference to the Stipulation. 


plaintiffs or any member of the Settlement Class (other than 
those who have validly requested exclusion therefrom), 


206 


whether directly, derivatively, representatively or in any other 
capacity, against any of the Released Persons or against any- 
one else in connection with or that arise now or hereafter out 
of the Merger Agreement, the Tender Offer, the Distribution 
Agreement, the Capital Contribution Agreement, the em- 
ployee compensation arrangements, the Tender Agreements, 
the Initial Proposed Settlement, this Settlement (except for 
compliance with the Settlement), or any matters, transactions 
or occurrences referred to in the December 4, 1990 Claim 
Letter and the Amended Complaint in the Action, or the fi- 
duciary or disclosure obligations of any of the Released Per- 
sons with respect to any of the foregoing, and including 
without limitation the claims asserted in the California Fed- 
eral Actions, the Second Minton Action and the Second Ep- 
stein Action (all as defined in the Stipulation) (all of which 
are hereinafter collectively referred to as the “Settled 
Claims”) are hereby compromised, settled, released and dis- 
charged with prejudice by virtue of the proceedings herein 
and this Order and Final Judgment. 

4. The plaintiffs and all members of the Settlement Class 
(other than those who have validly requested exclusion there- 
from), their successors and assigns, whether acting directly, 
derivatively, representatively or in any other capacity, are 
hereby permanently barred and enjoined from instituting or 
prosecuting any other action in any court of this or any other 
jurisdiction based upon, relating to, or for the purpose of en- 
forcing any of the Settled Claims. 


5. Plaintiffs’ attorneys of record herein are hereby awarded 
the sum of $250,000 in fees and reimbursement of expenses 
in connection with the Actions, which sum the Court finds to 
be fair and reasonable and which shall be paid to plaintiffs’ 
attorneys according to the terms set forth in the Stipulation. 
Said sum shall accrue interest and be paid in accordance with 
paragraph 8 of the Stipulation.
