Re: In re MCA Inc. Shareholders Litigation, 
Consol. C_A. No. 11740 (Del. Ch.) 


Dear Mr. Wachtell: 


This will summarize the principal grounds on which plain- 
tiffs intend to rely in seeking preliminary injunctive relief. 
The nature of the relief sought will also be described By 
agreement with you, this letter will substitute for the filing of 


ducted. In addition, plaintiffs further reserve the right to 
modify and/or supplement their legal analysis as the facts de- 
velop. 


To begin with, plaintiffs will argue that defendants have 
breached their fiduciary duties of complete candor in connec. 
tion with the tender offer materials issued by both MCA and 
Matsushita with respect to the tender offer. MCA has distri 
uted the Offer to Purchase to its shareholders together with 


212 


responsible for the inadequate disclosures provided to MCA 
shareholders in connection with the transaction. 


Plaintiffs further contend that the management arrange- 
ments regarding benefits and compensation are deceptively 
presented in the disclosure materials. For example, Mr. 
Sheinberg and other members of senior management who oc- 
cupy seats on the Board of Directors or exercise substantial 
responsibilities in managing and supervising MCA are to be 
provided with immense cash bonuses in connection with the 
acquisition in addition to receiving enormous increases in 
their basic compensation. These massive payments are pre- 
sented as substantially equivalent to the incentive compensa- 
tion that these officers/directors otherwise would have 
received in the ordinary course and are also presented as 
having been insisted upon by Matsushita as a means of assur- 
ing management continuity. Plaintiffs allege and will seek to 
demonstrate that the bonuses are far greater than their recipi- 
ents could have expected to receive in the ordinary course and 
that management insisted upon such benefits as the price of 
its willingness to negotiate with Matsushita. Plaintiffs will 
also seek to demonstrate that Matsushita willingly agreed to 
pay and may well have encouraged the payment of such com- 
pensation because of its expectation that this would vastly 
limit the independence of senior management and the Board 
of Directors in negotiating with Matsushita and thereby save 
the purchaser potentially hundreds of millions or billions of 
dollars in acquisition costs that a fully disinterested MCA 
Board would have insisted upon. The portrayal of the man- 
agement benefits as consistent with ordinary practice and as 
proceeding from the wishes of the purchaser to secure the 
services of the present officers and directors is materially false 
and deceptive and calculated to present a neutral or positive 
impression of special arrangements which create serious 
conflicts of interest that the parties have attempted to conceal. 


Plaintiffs also contend and will seek to demonstrate that 
Mr. Wasserman’s planned equity participation in “Holdings” 
following completion of the acquisition establishes that the 
parties are engaged in a form of “going-private” transaction 


213 


subject to the disclosure requirements of Rule 13e-3 of the 
Exchange Act. However, the parties have not made the re- 
quired filings with the SEC which would be made were they 
proceeding in accordance with Rule 13e-3. In particular, the 
Lazard Freres investment banker’s “book” has not been made 
publicly available as would otherwise be the case were the 
parties proceeding in accordance with the requirements for a 
going-private transaction. Material information regarding the 
fairness and adequacy of the proposed transaction has conse- 
quently been withheld from the investing public. Plaintiffs 
will argue that the Delaware duties of complete candor incor- 
porate the requirement to make available in connection with a 
— transaction the materials required by Rule 
e-3. 


_ Plaintiffs further contend that the defendants have mislead- 
ingly concealed their intention to retain effective control over 
WWOR-TV following the spinoff and prevent the emergence 
of market forces which could generate a sale of this division 
at a price representing genuinely maximum value for the 
shareholders. Although the tender offer materials appear to 
suggest that the television station will be open to bidders, the 
provision for a board of directors following the spinoff which 
will be dominated by members of MCA senior management 
and the planned implementation of a shareholder rights plan 
or “poison pill” in a form satisfactory to MCA and Matsushita 
have both the purpose and effect of entrenching in the control 
of WWOR-TV the MCA designees whom defendants actually 
seek to protect from any challenge to their control following 
the Matsushita takeover. The offering materials deceptively 
conceal these facts which are contrary to the fundamental ob- 
ligation of the defendants to seek and arrange the best possi- 
ble transaction in connection with the change in control. 


Plaintiffs also contend that the shareholders are being de- 
nied significant and material information regarding the operat- 
ing segments of MCA which has been provided to the 
purchaser but has not been shared with the stockholders on an 
equivalent basis. The offering materials claim that this infor- 
mation would not be “material” to shareholders but this is 


214 


simply a self-serving assertion which itself is false and mis- 
leading in view of the obvious importance of this information 
to shareholders seeking to make a reasoned election between 
accepting the purported benefits of the tender offer or a 
statutory appraisal proceeding. 


Finally, plaintiffs contend that the tender offer materials are 
seriously deficient in their failure to disclose prior market 
contacts between MCA and other potential purchasers which 
would indicate the level of market interest MCA could com- 
mand in an open and effective auction. Plaintiffs believe and 
will seek to demonstrate that other potential purchasers were 
rebuffed when they refused to provide the management 
benefits and other concessions to management to which Mat- 
sushita has acceded. Knowledge of those prior market con- 
tacts would, therefore. materially assist the shareholders in 
assessing the fairness and adequacy of the current proposal 
and in appreciating the significant extent to which conflicts of 
interest or potential conflicts have impeded the success of 
prior bids or expressions of interest. Shareholders are simi- 
larly entitled to be informed of other possible alternatives 
which could have been explored but were not. 


Plaintiffs further contend that the transaction 
violates defendants’ fundamental duties enunciated in Revion. 
Under Revion the MCA directors must act with the single 
objective of obtaining the highest possible price in connection 
with the sale of the corporation. The Court will find a viola- 
tion of Revion if the record demonstrates that the directors 
have not acted reasonably to attain this goal. See Roberts v. 
General Instrument Corp., Del. Ch., CA. No. 11639, Allen, 


C. (Aug. 13, 1990). 


While the burden is ordinarily upon the plaintiff to demon- 
strate that the directors have failed to act reasonably to attain 
this goal, the burden of proof must shift in this case because 
more than a majority of the directors have financial or other 
conflicting interests in the success of the transaction. Senior 
management are eligible to receive bonus and compensation 
payments immensely greater than they would otherwise 


215 


receive under their present contracts. Mr. Wasserman has ne- 
gotiated a very favorable arrangement in connection with the 
sale which will insulate him from costly capital gains taxes. 
Mr. Strauss lacks independence because of the dual represen- 
tation by his law firm of Matsushita and MCA and in fact ab- 
stained from voting on the transaction. Mr. Baker similarly 
lacks independence because of the substantial payments made 
to his law firm in connection with the transaction. In addition, 
Lazard’s “fairness” opinion is unreliable because of the 
agreement which Lazard has arranged by which Lazard re- 
ceives multi-million dollar advisory fees contingent upon the 
completion of the acquisition transaction. Mr. Rohatyn cannot 
claim any objectivity with respect to his support for the trans- 
action because of the multi-million dollar fees which Lazard 
will receive in the event that a sale is consummated. The re- 
maining directors who apparently have no financial stake in 
the transaction constitute a minority of the MCA Board.

sions of interest and while MCA may respond “yp

In contrast, the prior settlement contemplated certain thera- 
peutic changes in the rights plan of a spin-off entity known as 
Pinelands, which the Court found at that time problematic in 
value and of insufficient value to provide the predicate for a 
global settlement. 


A second salient difference between the [6] current settle- 
ment and the prior settlement is that class members who 
wished to exclude themselves from the binding effect of this 
settlement and any class action determination by the Court 
with respect to the settlement are afforded a right to opt out of 
the settlement pursuant to Chancery Court Rule 23(b)(3). This 
1S an Opt-out settlement case. 


The Court will recall that a great deal of time and effort 
was spent at the last hearing on the issue whether the settle- 
ment clause should be a non-opt-out or opt-out clause. There 
are cases in this Court that set forth very stringent standards 
and difficult criteria in that regard. Those issues no longer 
exist with respect to this settlement, as an opt-out right has 
been afforded. 

[7] 1 would add that in order to make the opt-out right 
maximally meaningful it was negotiated in connection with 
the settlement that not only would there be mailed notice, but 
that, uncommonly in these types of situations, there would 
also be a publication notice in the Wal] Street Journal, which 
did take place. This was done with the intention of assuring 
that the opt-out nght would be maximally meaningful in this 
situation. 


| would add that despite the number of objections that have 
come to the Court and the intensity of the rhetoric, there is no 
challenge here to the contents of either notice. There is no 
challenge to full disclosure in that notice And therefore there 
an be no question that those shareholders who have elected 
© Opt out or, alternatively, remain im the class — and while 
some have opted out. they are relatively smal!) in oumber 
There cam be no question that that was based on ful! informa- 
son. and that those who remained in the Court should deem : 
nformed consent

With respect to the procedural posture of the case, there is 
another fundamental difference as to where we are now as to 
where we were two years ago. The Court at that time was 
concerned that the federal securities claims which we sought 
to settle as part of the global settlement, claims asserted in 
California federal court under Rules 10b-13 and 14d-10 of the 
Securities Exchange Act, had not yet been adjudicated. In- 
deed, those cases were in a relatively early phase at that time. 
The Court concluded in its prior opinion that based on that 
lack of an adjudication, that those claims had what [8] the 
Court referred to as “arguable merit.” 


Those claims have now been adjudicated. They have been 
adjudicated on the basis of a full record, full discovery, ex- 
tensive motion practice and adversarial proceedings of the 
fullest extent. Plaintiffs in that case have been losers on each 
and every issue that they asserted in that proceeding. Their 
claims have failed. 


In brief, summary judgment has been granted by Judge 
Real with respect to the claims against Mr. Wasserman, Mr. 
Sheinberg, MCA. Judge Real concluded that there is no pri- 
vate right of action under SEC Rule 10b-13 and that the Was- 
serman transaction did not fall afoul of Rule 14d-10 because it 
was negotiated and entered into before the tender offer period 
and consummated thereafter. 


This ruling was based on findings of fact and stipulated 
facts and extensive discovery and a legal analysis of the cases, 
some of which were before your Honor at that time, and other 
subsequent authorities, such as the Kahn case in the Eastern 
District of Virginia and the Second Circuit affirmance in the 
Kramer case, al] of which convincingly demonstrate that a 
private transaction [9] entered into outside a tender offer pe- 
nod shall not be integrated with a tender offer to make the 
private transaction in any way subject to esther the Sithe pur- 
chase or best price provisions of 10b-13 and 14d-10 respec- 


uvely 


As significantly, if not more so, the federal plaintiffs in 
aliforma sought class certification. This was twice fully and 


227 


along and seek to represent those claims which the Epsteins 
the Rockfords and others in that case have failed in asserting. 


I might add that the plaintiffs in this case, in addition, were 
sanctioned by Judge Real for failure to provide necessary dis- 
covery in connection with class certification. Plaintiff Epstein 
was found to have testified falsely and under false pretenses 
in concealing in his deposition testimony that his shares were 
held in a tax-free account, free of tax consequences. Thus he 
had no standing to assert the so-called 10b-13 and 14d-10 
claims. Judge Real indicated that had he not dismissed the 
case in its entirety, Mr. Epstein would have been di i 
because of that fact alone. , —

Thus, the California plaintiffs have had [11] their day in 
court and their claims have been found to be lacking in sub-

peal to the Ninth Circuit. In our view, that does not affect the 
fairness of the settlement proposal we are making today. It is

the adverse adjudication of those claims, based on a legal

As before, the parties do seek a global settlement of estate 
claims and all related federal claims arising from the 
MCA/Matsushita acquisition. This Court’s power to effectu- 
ate such a global settlement and release of claims has already 
been determined to exist in this case and cannot be ques- 
tioned, as it is supported by wide-ranging authority of the Su- 
preme Court of this State, this Court’s own practice and 
federal courts who have honored state court releases of federal 
claims otherwise outside the jurisdiction of the state [12] 
court. As a policy matter Delaware courts have effectuated 
such global settlements based on the realistic assessment, as 
your Honor recognized in his prior opinion, that if there is to 
be any prospect of settlement at all in contested corporate liti- 
gation, over which this Court exercises wide and frequent ju- 
risdiction, there must be included the release and termination 
of any actual or potential claims arising from the same trans- 
action. This is a power routinely exercised in this Court. Your 
Honor has issued opinions in that area. Other judges of this 
Court have done the same. And cases involving federal claims 
which are pending in federal courts, which have been pending 
in federal appeals courts, have been terminated. 


In the Sithe-Energies case your Honor terminated a Cali- 
fornia Superior Court case that was arising from the same 
facts and circumstances as the settled Delaware matter. 


In the Becore Western case Vice Chancellor Berger termi- 
nated an action which was pending in a federal appeals court 
and raised federal securities claims based on the overall as- 
sessment that the global settlement and release of [13] all 
claims was fair in relation to the consideration supporting the 


settlement.

The Court found, as I indicated, no private right of action 
under 10b-13. That’s very substantially supported by that rule 
itself, which does not expressly or impliedly, as part of the 
scheme of which it is a part, in any way suggest the existence

[19] The Court found that the Wasserman transaction was 
negotiated and agreed to prior to the tender offer and per- 
formed thereafter. He did not integrate and resisted arguments 
to integrate the Wasserman transaction with the tender offer 
because as a factual matter they create separate obligations 
which were separately negotiated, and as a legal matter, as 
reflected in the relevant case law before him at that time and 
now reaffirmed by Kahn and Kramer and a whole slew of 
SEC releases and consistent authority in this area, a private 
transaction negotiated prior to a tender offer and performed 
thereafter, which is separate from the tender offer, is not in the 
tender offer and, therefore, is not subject to the peculiar con- 
straints of the tender offer rules. 


Judge Real looked at the Field v. Trump decision, and 
knew that in the Second Circuit’s affirmance in Kramer, a 
subsequent decision, Field was limited to the ruling as to 
when you would integrate two successive tender offers and 
determine whether a first one had been withdrawn and a sec- 
ond one was really part of the same thing, to determine 
whether private deals in the interim were or were [20] not 
afoul of the Sithe purchase rule. 


But that’s all that Field speaks to. It doesn’t speak to this 
situation. This is a privately negotiated separate transaction 
which the Court has ruled as a factual and legal matter is not 
subject to the constraints of 10b-13 and 14d-10. There is no 
case that provides support for the integration of those transac- 


tions.

Let me comment for a moment on the theory that there is 
no class. Class certification was denied. It has been appealed. 
It can be reversed on appeal. There are still class members. 
The notion that somehow, because the class certification has 
been denied, the statute of limitations has run is erroneous. | 
cite to [37] Edwards v. Boeing Vertol Co., 717 F.2d 761, a 


242 


1983 decision by the Third Circuit Court of Appeals, which 
held that where class certification is denied but that denial is 
appealed, the statute of limitations is tolled until there is a fi- 
nal determination on the issue of class certification. That 
doesn’t happen until the appeals are exhausted. | would spe- 
cifically refer the Court to pages 765 and 766 of that decision 
for the discussion of the Third Circuit. 


So the reality is, if the Ninth Circuit reverses, there is still a 
class in California. There are still people out there who could 
assert those federal claims. I think that’s important because | 
think in part that is the explanation of why this settlement 
from the defendants’ point of view is being pressed so hard. 


The proposed settlement -- strike that. 


Let me go back and just remind the Court of a little bit of 
the procedural history here. I will be briefer than Mr. Schul- 


man. 


The Delaware actions were filed and then the California 
actions were filed, and then there was a settlement struck 
between the defendants and [38] the plaintiff class in this ac- 
tion, and then the Delaware complaint here was amended to 
include the factual predicate for the federal litigation, and then 
they sought this Court’s approval of their proposed settle- 
ment. So, in short, once they found out there were federal 
claims, they quickly amended the complaint here in an effort 
to get their settlement through. | think that says a iot. 


The proposed settlement is worth slightly less than two 
cents per share. The plaintiffs’ counsel expect to receive over 
half a million dollars in fees, plus they have asked for some 
$116,000 of reimbursement for costs for their efforts in set- 
tling extremely weak State law claims and releasing federal 
law claims in which they have no interest. They don’t want to 
prosecute them, they didn’t press them, and they certainly 
couldn’t prosecute them in this Court, given the federal 
court’s exclusive jurisdiction. 


For $2 million the defendants purchased an insurance pol- 
icy, in effect, shielding them against potential liability that 


243 


could range from $780 million to perhaps as high as a billion 
and one-half. And they get to avoid dealing with the [39] 
counsel and the plaintiffs who are most interested in those 
claims, who have an interest in pressing those claims as a 
class action in federal court, who have litigated and continue 
to litigate vigorously those claims. 


I think this two-cent settlement is collusive. The facts of 
their case are a paradigm of the kind of collusion that this 
Court I believe as a matter of public policy -- indeed, as a 
matter of public trust -- should not sanction. Approval of set- 
tlements of this kind serve only to undermine public confi- 
dence in our legal system. And this Court understands that. 


I don’t think anyone here today fails to grasp the true na- 
ture of this two-cent settlement. I quote from Professor Cof- 
fee’s article, which was reproduced as an exhibit to the papers 
filed by Mrs. de Ruiz in objection: 


“Today, there may be a third form of collusion appearing 
on the horizon. Under it, the parties to one action (in state 
court) negotiate a non-merits based settlement in which at 
least one of defendants’ primary goals is to preclude plaintiffs 
in a different case (in federal court). In short, [40] one group 
of plaintiff attorneys effectively sell the legal claims raised 
by another group in order to enhance their own settlement at 
the expense of the absent group. 


That, your Honor, I submit is what is happening here. A 
year ago this Court held that the State law claims sought to be 
litigated by the plaintiffs here were substantially without 
merit, and the federal claims were the only claims that ap- 
peared to have substantive merit. 


I think what that means is, the plaintiffs here don’t have 
anything to sell to the defendants by way of settlement except 
the federal claims. And those claims aren’t raised here. They 
are raised in California. They are being sought to be settled 
here by way of a release to enhance the settlement here and at 
the expense of the California litigants, who would include 
Mrs. de Ruiz. 


244 


The defendants, too, benefit. They don’t have to deal with 
the California attorneys. They are the ones who have been 
willing to expend the time and the effort and the money, first 
at the trial level and now on appeal, to enforce what they be- 
lieve to be the class rights in connection with [41] these fed- 
eral claims. 


It’s no wonder the defendants would rather deal with the 
plaintiffs here. One need only compare the docket, which has 
over 500 entries in California, to the docket here. What's been 
done here: They filed a complaint, an amended complaint. 
They have taken three depositions over the course of two 
days. They have had one document production request. And 
that’s pretty much it, other than settlement. By comparison, in 
California they were almost on the eve of trial or certainly 
approaching trial in California when the Court ruled on the 


summary judgment motion. 


The federal claims have material value. I think the defen- 
dants’ and the plaintiffs’ own papers amply demonstrate that 
fact. The defendants say the federal claims have no merit. 
There can be no class action to pursue those claims because 
the statute of limitations has run. Why do they want to settle 
them here? Why do they want a release? If they are correct, if 
they believe that, they wouldn’t need a release. 


They say the only people who can pursue the federal claims 
are the individual named [42] plaintiffs in the California ac- 
tion, and I suppose those who have opted out. All others are 
barred because of the statute of limitations. At least that’s 
their argument, as I read it. 


We know that argument is wrong. The Third Circuit tells 
us that the statute of limitations continues to be tolled until 
there’s a final adjudication. So if there is a reversal, there is a 
possibility that there will be a class action in California. 


Why do they need a release of the federal claims? They 
have no faith in their position. I can only surmise that they are 
afraid that Judge Real will be reversed. He does have the 


245 


highest reversal rate, apparently, of any District Court judge 
sitting in California. 


I think they fear the prospect of having to litigate the Cali- 
fornia action on its merits, so they want a release. They cer- 
tainly are not afraid of the State law claims, because this 
Court has already told them that they have little or nothing to 
fear with respect to those claims. 


We aren’t suggesting that this Court second-guess the fed- 
eral District Court in [43] California or attempt to predict 
what the Ninth Circuit will do, and it was not for that reason 
that we submitted the appeal briefs filed in California. We 
submitted those really for only two reasons: to show that the 
plaintiffs in the California actions were seriously litigating 
material federal claims and that briefing before the Ninth Cir- 
cuit was complete. 


This Court should not ignore the posture of the federal liti- 
gation. It has gone forward. The defendants admit that there 
was substantial discovery in that action, and I think the appel- 
late briefs show that the plaintiffs there are pressing hard on 
their claims. And I think if your Honor reads those briefs, not 
for the purpose of second-guessing either the District Court or 
predicting what the Ninth Circuit will do, but reads those 
briefs with a view to assessing whether there are meritorious 
claims still alive in California, I think you will reach the con- 
clusion that those claims are meritorious and they are worth 
more than two cents a share. 


Several years ago Chancellor Allen faced a similar problem 
in Stepak v. Tracinda Corp. There [44] the Chancellor was 
faced with a proposed settlement that offered 12 to 13 cents 
per share, five cents after attorneys’ fees and expenses were 
deducted. And that proposed settlement, if approved, would 
have settled claims being litigated in the California State 
courts. Chancellor Allen had this to day, and I quote: 


“ 


.. it is not necessary to engage in a subtle or detailed 
analysis of the expected value of such claims as the class may 
possess in order to conclude that this proposal is not worthy 


246 


of serious consideration. Only were the Court able to con- 
clude that those claims have only nuisance value could the 
proposed settlement conceivably be approved.” 


That’s at pages 13 and 14 of his opinion. 


The Chancellor further concluded that the five cents per 
share settlement value represented, and I quote, “... an implicit 
agreement by the parties negotiating it that the case was to- 
tally without merit.” That appears at page 12. 


The Chancellor went on and said something else that | 
think is very significant. He said, “Rule 23(e) mandates sub- 
stantive court review, [45] in part at least, because the class 
action form of action does make possible opportunistic behav- 
ior at the expense of absent class members. Where there are 
two or more attorneys purporting to act on behalf of the same 
or overlapping classes, there is a special risk that a defendant 
will seek advantage in choosing the adversary with whom it 
will negotiate, and a risk that the blessed plaintiff will be ac- 
commodating in exchange for an agreement that includes le- 
gal fees.” Page 15 of the Chancellor’s opinion. 


And finally, one more passage from that case, and I quote: 
“While I would not say without a more thorough investigation 
that such conduct” -- referring to possible sell-out of a class -- 
“has in fact occurred here, I note that the history of the prose- 
cution of these matters seems consistent with that possibility. 
In all events, candor does impel me to say that the proposed 
settlement, alone among the many such applications I have 
been required to review, engenders disdain that is pronounced 
and lingers. It is frankly unimaginable that I could exercise 
my judgment to approve it as fair to the class. Moreover, ap- 
proval of such a proposal would, [46] in my opinion, threaten 
to hold the class action mechanism up to justifiable scorn and 
to charges, too frequently made without adequate grounds, 
that the stockholder class action mechanism represents noth- 
ing .so much as a device for lawyers to enrich themselves 
while serving no practical interest of those for whom they are 


charged to act.” That appears at page 16. 


247 


_ For that reason the Chancellor stayed the Delaware litiga- 
mn tee ty oe litigated California case could 
roceed, and he refused to even schedule a heari 
proposed settlement. ere

in the Karasik case, “... the amount claimed is one hundred 
million dollars and the amount received in settlement is a 
minimum of three hundred and eighty-five thousand dollars. 
Now that is a wide disparity. But it is one thing to assert a 


claim and another thing to prove the claim to j Fur 
thermore, it is one thing to obtain a judgment, and quite an 
other thing to collect it. Figures, however ing, should

[57] This Court is understanding of that logic. It has ap-

We have here a $2 million settlement. it doesn't matter 
how many shares are outstanding. One can divide it as many 
times as they want. If we had a $10 million settlement they 
would say it is seven cents a share. If we had a $20 million 
settlement they would say it is 30 conts 0 share. Where does it 
stop 


That's not the way this Court evaluates settlements. The 
Court looks at what's being made available to the class 
through the settlement and it makes a probabilistic assessment 
of what is coming to the class potentially through the litiga- 
tion. It doesn’t adjudicate claims, and it looks and makes this 
analysis through the optic of favoring settlements and recog- 
nizing that this allows people to get on with their affairs, to 
allow the courts to move on with their affairs, and t~ put liti- 
gation and weak claims to rest or to allow claims to be re- 
leased in present money instead of burdening the courts with 
continued litigation where there's no real prospect of success.

knowledge and involvement in the mailing of the notice. His 
sworn affidavit is the only competent evidence before this 
Court on the issue of notice. 


The undisputed facts regarding notice are simply as fol- 
lows. None of them are disputed by Dean Witter. Dean Witter 
received the notice of pendency on November 27. That’s the 
Exhibit C to Marian Minton’s memorandum, a letter from 
Richard Dole of Dean Witter. 


Pursuant to that same notice, Dean Witter had 14 days to 
contact Simpson Thacher if it wished more copies of the no- 
tice of pendency. Dean Witter failed to do so. In fact, by their 
own admission in the letters that Mr. Smith has submitted this 
morning, they didn’t call Simpson Thacher for the first time 
until December 15. That’s Dean Witter’s negligence. 


As stated in the Fan affidavit, the only competent evidence 
before your Honor on that point this morning, Mr. Fan and 
Shannon Strickland of Dean Witter exchanged two or three 
phone calls. [67] Those phone calls consisted of messages 
from Ms. Strickland asking Mr. Fan to return the call. Each 
time he failed promptly to do so. None of those messages 
were themselves requests for copies of the notice. 


On December 23 Dean Witter sent by regular mail a list of 
shareholders to Simpson Thacher. They sent them regular 
mail. But notwithstanding the fact December 23 is the day 
before Christmas Eve, that cover letter of December 23 does 
not arrive at Simpson Thacher until December 28. Enclosed is 
a list of Dean Witter shareholders purportedly customers or 
stockholders of MCA. 


Mr. Fan immediately calls Shannon Strickland and tells her 
that, “In light of the fact that you have given us a list of 
shareholders’ names rather than mailing labels, it would 
probably be more expeditious for us to send you the 750 cop- 
ies and have you mail them directly yourselves.” 


Ms. Strickland agreed, and Mr. Fan sent those notices out, 
750 copies, by Federal Express that same day. Dean Witter 


259 


got them the next day. Then Dean Witter waited another eight 
days to bother [68] to mail those notices. 


Mr. Smith said he’s not interested in finding fault in the 
notice process. Small wonder, because the only fault here is 
Dean Witter’s. The case law of this Court makes clear that a 
shareholder who elects the convenience of holding stock in 
street name bears all attendant risks. Mr. Smith's vague com- 
plaints about the way the system works notwithstanding, 
that’s the law. 


Add to this the fact that notice was published in the Wall 
Street Journal on November 18. The Fan affidavit stands un- 
contradicted by any competent evidence. There’s absolutely 
no question that the notice was given in accordance with this 
Court’s order of October 27. 


Thank you.

CORPORATE SECURITIES 
John C. Coffee Jr. 


Suspect Settlements 
in Securities Litigation 


MUCH LIKE THE production of Sausage and legislation, 
the settlement of securities litigation is a process that the 
high-minded are best advised not to examine too closely. 
For some time, commentators have recognized that serious 
principal/agent problems compromise the utility of 
shareholder litigation as a mechanism for deterring 
managerial misconduct.' Sometimes, the result can be 
collusive settlements in which low recoveries are 
exchanged for high plaintiffs’ attorneys fees. But, while 
anecdotal evidence has always been available that 
corroborated this pattern, aggregate statistical data has 
generally been lacking. Now, however, it has begun to 
emerge in two recent studies by professors at Yale and 
Stanford Law Schools.’ At the same time, greater judicial 
awareness of the potential for collusive settlements is also 
creeping into judicial opinions -- particularly in a string of 
recent cases that have explicitly abandoned the lodestar 


method of compensating the plaintiff s attorney in class and 
securities litigation.’ 


A Subtle Process 


Collusion is, however, a complex phenomenon. It does 
not occur through naked exchanges of low recoveries for 
high fees, but by subtler processes that produce settlements 
not based on an action’s litigation merits. Thus, for the 
litigator, the most interesting recent development in 
securities litigation may be the appearance of a new 
technique by which a collusive settlement -- that is, a non- 
adversarial settlement that maximizes the attorney’s 
interests at the expense of the client’s -- can be negotiated. 
Last month, the First Circuit Court of Appeals ruled in 
Nottingham Partners v. Trans-Lux Corporation’ that a class 
action brought in federal court for securities law violations 


276 


was barred by the settlement of a later-filed state class 
action, even though the state class action did not (and could 
not) raise any securities law claims. 


Because the general release issued to the defendants in 
the state court settlement resulted in issue preclusion, the 
consequence was that no court, state or federal, could hear 
plaintiffs’ claims of proxy fraud. The seeming paradox in 
Nottingham Partners is that a state court settlement can 
have preclusive effect despite the fact that the state court 
lacked subject matter jurisdiction over the claims thereby 


barred. 


WHILE THE LEGAL issue of when one court has the 
power to approve the release of claims pending in another 
forum is complex, the practical message to plaintiffs’ 
counsel is much simpler: weak derivative and class action 
claims that once would have been routinely dismissed by 
the Delaware Chancery Court now have an enhanced 
settlement value, because they can confer immunity with 
respect to pending federal securities class actions, which 
often would have a significant settlement value. 


Ironically, the incentives for defendants are even clearer: 
never seek dismissal on the merits of a state class action if a 
companion federal class action raising related federal 
claims is possible, but instead reach a settlement in which 
the defendants are given a general release. If properly 
structured to fall within the Nottingham Partners precedent, 
such a release will preclude a federal class action raising 
federal securities claims. 


This type of settlement can be described as “collusive” 
because the parties to the state court settlement are 
effectively depriving absent players -- the plaintiffs and 
their attorneys in the federal action -- of their day in court. 
The net result is effectively both a wealth transfer from the 
federal plaintiffs’ attorneys to the state plaintiffs’ attorneys 
and a reduction in the total recovery to the class (because 


277 


the state plaintiffs’ attorneys are settling federal claims that 
they can never win, but can only settle, and should thus 
accept a lesser settlement than would the federal plaintiffs’ 
attorneys). 


Although it is still premature to predict that Rule 10b-5 
actions will become an endangered species, the possible end 
result of Nottingham Partners will be that the already well- 
travelled road to the Delaware courts could become a six 
lane highway as plaintiffs and defendants rush to settle 
there before a federal court can stay them. This column will 
analyze the inter-jurisdictional race to the courthouse that 
Nottingham Partners opens up -- after first surveying the 
new empirical research on the settlement stage in securities 
litigation. 

The Settlement Stage 


To study shareholder litigation, Professor Roberta 
Romano of Yale Law School developed a sample of 535 
public corporations, randomly chosen from the New York 
Stock Exchange, NASDAQ and from firms that delisted 
from those markets. Studying these firms from 1966 
through 1987, she identified 139 shareholder actions (both 
class actions and derivative suits) against 99 firms. While 
the incidence of litigation was low (NYSE firms could 
expect to be sued only once every 42 years on this basis), 
av substantial rise in litigation frequency during 


Her disposition data confirms earlier studies: plaintiffs 
rarely win, but most actions do settle. In fact, 65 percent of 
these actions settled, but only in two suits (1.5 percent of 
her total sample) did plaintiffs win a judgment. This 
translates into a 6 percent success rate for plaintiffs in terms 
of cases reaching court adjudication.® Shareholder 
settlements, she concluded, had two striking features: First, 
only half of all settlements involved a monetary recovery 
(46 of 83). Second, fee awards were paid to plaintiffs 
attorneys in over 90 percent of her cases (75 of 83), even 
though the plaintiffs received a monetary recovery in only 


278 


around half.” Indeed, in seven cases (8 percent), the only 
relief was plaintiff attorney's fees.” 


A much criticized aspect of shareholder litigation has 
been the use of nonpecuniary settlements that afford no 
monetary relief to the shareholders or corporation, but 
which are used to justify an award of plaintiffs’ attorneys 
fees. Critics have not infrequently suggested that such relief 
is often cosmetic and intended largely to hype the value of 
the settlement to justify a fee award.'' In her sample, 
Professor Romano found 25 settlements affording structural 
relief, but only four of these had a monetary recovery." She 
concluded that the value of such structural relief seemed 
“inconsequential,” and “for the most part, board 
composition changes are cosmetic.”'> Meanwhile, attorneys 
fees and expenses, which were awarded in 75 cases in her 
study, averaged $908,808. '* 


Professor Romano’s data suggest that the problem of 
collusion is considerably more pronounced in derivative 
actions than in class actions. The average recovery in 
derivative actions ($6,056,172) was about half that in class 
actions ($10,948,279), and a cash payout to shareholders 
occurred in only 21 percent of derivative actions, but in 67 
percent of class actions.'* Attorneys’ fees were also lower 
in derivative actions ($394,771) than in class actions 
($1,476,596),'° which may reflect a judicial estimate of the 
lower value of the plaintiffs’ attorneys’ services in 
derivative suits. 


Some may interpret this data to mean that derivative suits 
are largely frivolous. Others may see this data as showing 
that the procedural obstacles (in particular, the demand rule) 
have been placed so high in Delaware as to make even 
meritorious cases unwinnable. As a result, plaintiffs can 
hope only to achieve a small settlement. Yet, what both 
sides in this debate must recognize is that settlement (not 
adjudication) is the primary mode of resolution. Moreover, 
courts seem unable to play more than a weak monitoring 
role in supervising this stage, because settlements providing 


279 


little discernible benefit to the corporation or its 
shareholders frequently pass judicial muster. 


The pressures to settle are particularly strong in 
derivative actions for two reasons: First, corporate law 
generally forbids the indemnification of litigation expenses 
if the corporate official is adjudicated to have breached a 
duty owed to the corporation, and D&O insurance contains 
eee Fa eannnaty wide eon eine Uhaly t 

icable i action is resolved aj t 
than if it is settled. —— 

Second, defense counsel maintains that a substantial cost 
differential favors plaintiff's attorneys, who can carry the 
action forward at low cost to themselves while imposing 
high costs on the defendants (and ultimately the 


percent of the firms that were sued in her study accounted 
for roughly half of all suits.'’ Moreover, those firms 
were exposed to multiple suits did appear to settle at a 
marginally higher rate, and firms that were sued only once 
appear to have settled at a lower monetary recovery than 
those that were repetitively sued. Although these results 
were not statistically significant, the hypothesis that firms 
that settle easily are recurrently sued is at least consistent 
with this data. 

Although the pressures to settle are more intense in the 
derivative suit context (and the value of the relief to the 
corporation justifying the award of attorneys’ fees 

; y more suspect), similar problems are 
evident in securities class actions. Indeed, a 1991 study of 
Securities class actions by Professor Janet Cooper 


eS 


280 


Alexander of Stanford Law School casts even more doubt 
on whether the settlement process leads to outcomes that 
reflect the action’s merits." 


Studying a specially selected sample of securities class 
actions involving computer-related initial public offerings 
during the first half of 1983, she discovered that (1) every 
single company in the industry whose stock declined in the 
six months following its initial public offering was sued, 
and (ii) the resulting cases uniformly settled for 
approximately one-quarter of the potential damages. As a 
result, she concludes that “the strength of the plaintiffs’ 
case on the merits did not appear to be a significant factor 
in determining the outcome” of the cases she studied. 


Why are the merits and the likely outcome at trial 
seemingly irrelevant to the settlement award? Professor 
Alexander points to a variety of factors: extremely risk- 
averse defendants, astronomic potential damages, the 
lodestar formula for awarding attorneys fees, and the 
existence of legal rules governing insurance and 
indemnification that encourage the parties to settle on any 
basis, rather than go to trial. 


For example, a plaintiff's attorney who is compensated 
on a contingent hourly basis and who has invested a year or 
more in the preparation of an action has little, if any, 
economic reason to go to trial, when the potentially higher 
recovery to the class at trial will not substantially benefit 
him (because it will add only marginally to the hours that 
can be billed under the lodestar formula). 


Professor Alexander’s thesis is a provocative and radical 
one that contradicts the standard “law and economics” 
orthodoxy, which assumes that the parties to litigation settle 
based on their rational estimates of the expected outcome at 
trial.”’ Still, she may overstate. What she describes as the 
irrelevance of the merits may in fact be better described as 
the consequence of a unique statutory tilt toward the 
plaintiff in the Securities Act of 1933. 


281 


In effect, the statutory law has made the usual factual 
merits irrelevant. Because initial public offerings are 
governed by Section 11 of the Securities Act of 1933, 
which places the burden on most defendants to prove non- 
negligence and holds the issuer strictly liable for any 
material misstatement or omission, there may be few 
legitimate issues for a trial to resolve. 


In this light, the governing formula she describes under 
which all securities class actions are settled at 25 percent of 
their requested damages may simply reflect defendants’ 
judgment that these cases are unwinnable; the settlement 
then is largely derived by discounting the damage claim by 
the time value of money over the interval between the 
actual settlement date and the future point at which a trial 
would eventually award full damages to the plaintiffs. 


As she acknowledges, the Securities Act of 1933 may 
give plaintiffs a virtual “put” for the period of the statute of 
limitations, enabling them to exchange their securities for 
their original purchase price. If so, the problem lies less 
with the settlement system than with the substantive law, 
itself, which in this unique area arguably tilts excessively in 
the direction of the plaintiff. 


These two studies by Professors Romano and Cooper 
point in opposite directions. The Romano study suggests 
that stockholder litigation produces scant benefits for 
shareholders, while the Cooper study seemingly finds that 
securities class actions produce excessive and unjustified 
benefits. While defendants may agree with each of their 
critiques, they cannot both be right across the board: the 
problem cannot simultaneously be that stockholders get too 
little and too much. Still, both their stories have one 
plausible theme in common: the settlement process is not 


working. 


282 


Faces of Collusion 


As traditionally understood, a collusive settlement was 
one in which the plaintiffs’ attorneys sell out their clients in 
return for an overly generous fee award. The Romano study 
supports this traditional understanding of the problem. In 
contrast, the Cooper study suggests that defendants can also 
collude, by passing the costs of the settlement on to 
different absent parties: namely, the insurers, or the 
indemnifying corporation. Conceptually, the process is the 
same: the immediate participants maximize their interests at 
the expense of the absent parties. 


Today, there may be a third form of collusion appearing 
on the horizon. Under it, the parties to one action (in state 
court) negotiate a non-merits based settlement in which at 
least one of defendants’ primary goals is to preclude 
plaintiffs in a different case (in federal court). In short, one 
group of plaintiffs’ attorneys effectively sell the legal 
claims raised by another group in order to enhance their 
own settlement at the expense of the absent, group. This is 
the problem raised by Nottingham Partners. 


In March 1986, Trans-Lux issued a proxy statement 
whereby it sought to a adopt a series of “shark repellent” 
charter amendments. Shortly after these charter 
amendments were approved, Trans-Lux announced a sale of 
24 of its movie theaters for $15 million. 


Two years later, the plaintiffs in Nottingham brought suit 
in federal court, alleging that the non-disclosure of the 
impending sale violated Rule 14a-9 because its 
announcement in the proxy statement would have 
effectively put Trans-Lux on the auction block and caused 
shareholders to reject the proposed shark repellents. 


Shortly thereafter, a parallel class action was filed in 
Delaware Chancery Court, asserting various violations of 
state law (including disclosure shortcomings in the 1986 
proxy statement).~ Within three months of its filing, a 
proposed settlement was reached in the Delaware action, 


2483 


and, not surprisingly, it provided for a non-pecuniary 
settlement (but also for the payment of relatively modest 
plaintiffs’ attorneys fees of $120,000)" The federal 
plaintiffs objected to the settlement, but the Delaware 
Chancery Court certified the class, overruled their 
objections, and approved the settlement. 


On appeal, the federal plaintiffs also raised an important 
constitutional argument before the Delaware Supreme 
Court: that due process entitled them to an opportunity to 
opt out of the Delaware class action” In Phillips Petroleum 
Co. v. Shutts,”® the Supreme Court held in 1985 that before 
a State court may bind an absent plaintiff in a class action 
“concerning a claim for money damages or similar relief at 
law,” it must provide such a plaintiff “with an opportunity 
to remove himself from the class action by executing and 
returning an ‘opt-out’ or ‘request for exclusion’ form to the 
court.” Yet, on appeal, the Delaware Supreme Court 
distinguished Shutts by finding that the right to opt out did 
not apply to a class action that primarily sought equitable or 
injunctive relief, even if the action also sought monetary 
relief. In such circumstances, it said, the Chancery Court 
could permit opting out as a discretionary matter, but was 
under no obligation to do so.” 


When Trans-Lux thereafter pleaded the release given to it 
in the Delaware settlement as a defense to the federal 
action, the district court and later the First Circuit agreed 
with the Delaware Supreme Court’s analysis.” Other 
decisions have similarly found that the right to opt out does 
not apply to class actions primarily seeking injunctive 
relief. Yet, in sharp contrast to Delaware, the New York 
Court of Appeals has ruled this year that, although no right 
to opt out need be given from that portion of a class action 
seeking injunctive relief, a plaintiff must be permitted to opt 
out with respect to its claim for monetary relief.’ 


While New York’s rule is constitutionally sounder, 
Delaware’s approach poses a major hurdle for securities 
plaintiffs, because it is easily manipulated. Indeed, the facts 


284 


of the Trans-Lux litigation illustrate this potential for abuse 
in the artificiality of the equitable relief sought in that case. 


Plaintiffs in the state action sought an injunction 
cancelling Trans-Lux’s charter amendments two years after 
their adoption, when the issue of the possible sale of the 
company was long since moot. If this is sufficient to 
characterize a class action as primarily equitable in 
character, then even plaintiffs who have filed an earlier 
action for monetary damages in federal court are in trouble, 
because a Delaware action can always be brought for 
injunctive relief and will predictably settle quickly in order 
to be used as a barrier to the federal action. 


For the federal plaintiffs in such a case, the exceedingly 
difficult procedural issue will be whether to seek the right 
to opt out in Delaware (which is discretionary with the 
Delaware court) or to avoid Delaware entirely and later 
claim in federal court that they were not adequately 
represented in the Delaware action.” But so long as the 
state court action involves an “identical operative factual 
predicate” (which in securities cases will be the norm 
when both actions allege disclosure violations), it is now 
the law that claims over which a federal court has exclusive 
jurisdiction may be released in state court. At this point, at 
least until the Supreme Court clarifies Shuts, the prospect 
for collusion is clear and present. 


Conclusion 


Many courts have unthinkingly uttered the “familiar 
axiom that a bad settlement is almost always better than a 
good trial.”’> This column has contended that not only is 
this assertion shallow at best, but it is coming to mask a 
basic failure in securities litigation. The prospects for 
reform will be the focus of my next column. 


285

[D.2.] 


(a) It is unlikely that the Ninth Circuit will 
find that SEC Rules 10b-13 and 14d-10 


provide a private right of action 


The Federal District Court specifically held in the Califor- 
nia Federal Actions that there is no private right of action un- 
der Rule 10b-13. See Amended Order Granting Motions to 
Dismiss, dated February 20, 1992, Appendix, Exh. E. Recent 
United States Supreme Court and Ninth Circuit authority have 
“drastically narrow[ed]” the bases upon which private rights 
of action will be implied and such rights are highly 
“disfavor{ed].”"° Therefore, it is doubtful that the Federal 
Plaintiffs will succeed in reinstating their claims on appeal as 
the Federal District Court has already held that there is no 
private right of action under SEC Rule 10b-13 and the weight 
of authority appears to suggest that neither SEC Rule 10b-13 
nor Rule 14d-10 authorizes a private right of action. 


First, the plain language of Rule 10b-13 does not state or 
imply that such a right of action was intended and there is no 
compelling evidence that the SEC intended or even consid- 
ered the possibility that such a private right of action be avail- 
able under that Rule. In fact, under § 21(d)(1) of the 
Securities Exchange Act of 1934, the Rule is enforced by the 
SEC through injunctive actions, thereby further reinforcing 
that the SEC did not contemplate that a private remedy also 
be available.’ Moreover, pursuant to Rule 10b-13(d), the 


° Fidelity Fin. Corp. v. Federal Home Loan Bank, 589 F. Supp. 885, 891- 
92 (N.D. Cal 1983), aff'd, 792 F.2d 1432 (9th Cir. 1986), cert. denied, 
479 U.S. 1064 (1987); accord California v. Sierra Club, 451 U.S. 287, 
297 (1981) (“[t}he federal judiciary will not engraft a remedy on a statute, 
no matter how salutary, that Congress did not intend to provide”). 


'' See Karahalios v. National Federation of Federal Employees, Local 
1263, 489 U.S. 527, 533 (1989), Middlesex County Sewerage Authority v. 
National Sea Clammers Ass'n, 453 U.S. 1, 15 (1981). 


294 


SEC is expressly permitted to exempt certain transactions 
from Rule 10b-13, which power is seemingly inconsistent 
with any implied private right of action. Therefore, it is un- 
likely that the Federal Plaintiffs will be able to carry their 
heavy burden of proving that, contrary to the Federal District 
Cc ourt’s ruling, a private right of action exists under Rule 1 0b- 
13. 


Second, with respect to Rule 14d-10, in order for the Ninth 
Circuit to find that there is an implied private right of action 
thereunder, it must find that the Rule was promulgated pursu- 
ant to a section of the Securities Exchange Act of 1934 as to 
which Congress intended a private remedy to exist. See Ernst 
& Ernst v. Hochfelder, 425 U.S. 185, 213-14 (1976). In this 
regard, Rule 14d-10 was promulgated pursuant to §§ 14(d)(6) 
and 14(d)(7) of the Exchange Act of 1934 which, under cur- 
rent analysis, would also not appear to implicate a private 
right of action. It does not seem that the Federal Plaintiffs will 
be able to satisfy their heavy burden of demonstrating that 
Congress intended such a private remedy because, inter alia, 
the plain language of those sections does not confer rights 
upon private parties. To the contrary, §§ 14(d)(6) and 14(d)(7) 
simply note the requirements with which an entity making a 
tender offer must comply in order to lawfully make such an 
offer. Furthermore, the legislative history of those sections is 


? See, e.g., Suter v. Artist M., 112 S. Ct. 1360, 1370 (1992)(“the burden is 
on respondents [i.e., the Federal Plaintiffs] to demonstrate that Congress 
intended to make a private remedy available. . .”), /n re Washington Pub- 
lic Power Supply System Securities Litig., 823 F.2d 1349, 1353 (9th Cir. 
1987)“Plaintiffs must establish that Congress intended to provide for the 
private remedy which they ask us to imply. . .”). See also Jabion v. Dean 
Witter & Co., 614 F.2d 677, 680 (9th Cir. 1980)(court declined to find 
private right of action under § 6(b) of the Securities Exchange Act of 
1934\quoting Touche Ross & Co. v. Redington, 442 U.S. 560, 569 
(1979)). Given the stringent analysis that federal courts presently employ 
in implying private rights of action, the Second Circuit has recently ex- 
pressed doubt that a private right of action exists under Rule 10b-13. See 
Field v. Trump, 850 F.2d 938, 946 n.3 (2d Cir. 1988), cert. denied, 489 
U.S. 1012 (1989). 


295 


silent as to the creation of a private remedy thereunder. In 
addition, the statutory scheme into which Congress put §§ 
14(d)(6) and (d)(7) expressly provides for injunctive enforce- 
ment by the SEC pursuant to § 21(d)(1) of the Securities Ex- 
change Act of 1934 which, as with Rule 10b-13, is 
inconsistent with any implied private right of action under 
those sections. These facts would appear to suggest that the 
Ninth Circuit may well conclude that no private right of ac- 
tion exists under Rule 14d-10. See Northwest Airlines, Inc. v. 
Transport Workers Union, 451 U.S. 77, 94 n.31 (1981).

c. It is doubtful that the Ninth Circuit will 
overturn the Federal District Court’s 
factual finding that Mr. Wasserman 
did not receive higher consideration 
than the other MCA shareholders in 
the Tender Offer 


In its Second Amended Order, the Federal District Court 
concluded as follows: 


The value of the preferred stock received by Mr. 
Wasserman in exchange for his MCA common 
stock was no greater than the $66 in cash paid pur- 
suant to the Tender Offer. At best the basis of the 
Wasserman MCA stock would be transferred to the 
stock of Matsushita received by Wasserman under § 
368 (A)(1)(b) of the Internal Revenue Code. 


Second Amended Order at 8 (emphasis added). The Federal 
District Court further noted that, contrary to the contentions 
of the Federal Plaintiffs, Wasserman did not in fact receive a 
tax-free transaction, but rather a transfer of tax basis to his 
newly- acquired preferred stock upon which he (or his heirs) 
will have to pay taxes when it is eventually redeemed. /d at 
19. 


296 


Thus, while the Federal District Court’s conclusion that the 
Wasserman transaction did not occur during the Tender Offer 
period renders this point irrelevant, the Ninth Circuit is not 
likely to overturn the Federal District Court’s finding, based 
on all of the evidence before it, see, e.g., Declaration of Brad- 
ford Cornell dated Dec. 26, 1990, and filed in Epstein v. 
MCA, et al., CV90-6451-R (C.D. Cal.), at ¥¥ 8, 10 (Appendix, 
Exh. F), that the applicable Rules were not violated as Was- 
serman did not receive higher consideration for his shares 
than the other MCA shareholders. Instead, he received illiquid 
preferred stock tailored to his “unique tax situation” whose 
lack of negotiability and relative risk, among other factors, 
more than sufficiently outweigh whatever tax benefits Was- 
serman may have derived. See Second Amended Order at 8, 
11, 14-15; Appendix, Exh. F.

AFFIDAVIT OF ROGER W. KIRBY 


ROGER W. KIRBY, being duly sworn, says: 


1. | am a member of Messrs. Kaufman Malchman Kauf- 
mann & Kirby, counsel for plaintiff Espstein herein, and 
submit this affidavit in support of our motion for summary 
judgment and concerning a possible Delaware release. 


2. During the period December 3 to the morning of De- 
cember 7, 1990, Jeff Squire of my firm and I variously spoke 
to lawyers working for two of the four lead counsel firms in 
the Delaware action. We were told in words or in substance 
by these lawyers that they were pessimistic about their pros- 
pects for prevailing in Delaware and that defendants had 
shown no inclination even to discuss settlement. 


299 


3. It was during this period that our firm first learned of the 
organization of the Delaware case. Prior to then we had never 
received notice of an organization of the Delaware action ap- 
pointing Delaware lead counsel and not until Friday, Decem- 
ber 7, 1990, did we obtain, after our request therefor, a copy 
of the order organizing that litigation. The order, Exhibit A 
hereto, expressly restricts lead counsels’ authority to the 
Delaware litigation (“Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead Counsel shall set 
policy for plaintiffs for prosecution of this [Delaware] litiga- 
tion....” -- Exhibit A, 4 5) (emphasis supplied). 


4. Around 6:00 p.m. on Friday, December 7, 1990, David 
Bershad of Milberg Weiss Bershad et al. called me to say that 
defendants had now agreed to settle the Delaware action but 
that our agreement to fold our federal action into the Dela- 
ware was a condition precedent. Later that night, I received by 
fax the letter that I have attached hereto as Exhibit B. 


5. Over the weekend and on Monday, our firm researched 
the issues raised by that letter and further researched the is- 
sues raised in our pleadings in California. We concluded that 
Delaware counsel was clearly incorrect both in belittling the 
federal claims and in believing that they would be able to 
fashion a release in Delaware that could legally dispose of the 
federal claims. Accordingly, on Monday, December 10, 1990, 
I reported our conclusions to Bershad. 


6. Also on December 10, 1990, I had a conversation with 
one of Matsushita’s New York lawyers, Barry Ostrager, who 
said that perhaps I had not seen a so-called amendment to the 
Delaware action pleadings that purported to embrace our fed- 
eral claims. He then sent along a letter dated December 4, 
1990 that Bershad had sent to defendants’ counsel. This letter 
is the same as appears at Exhibit 2 to the Rotstein Declaration 
that defendants submitted as part of their December 11, 1990 
papers and Exhibit C hereto. 


7. We have in the last several days obtained copies of the 
docket in the Delaware action. A copy of the most recent ver- 


sion of that document in our possession is attached to this af- 
fidavit as Exhibit D. That docket reveals that neither the 


300 


December 4, 1990 letter nor any other so-called amended 
complaint by plaintiffs has been made part of the record of 
that case. The docket further reveals that none of the Japanese 
entities is a party to the Delaware proceeding. Federal Docu- 
ment Retrieval advised me at 11:30 a.m. this morning that no 
additions to the attached document have been made. 


8. We believe that the terms of the Delaware settlement are 
that some of the defensive mechanisms surrounding the 
WWOR equity stub to be issued to all MCA shareholders, 
including Wasserman, who tender their MCA shares will be 
dismantled, which, it is claimed, will enhance one value of the 
stub. To the extent that this settlement confers real value upon 
the class, it confers the most upon Wasserman individually. 


9. We have asked defendants to provide us with the memo- 
randum embodying the settlement (Exhibit E hereto) and have 
asked whether or not they have sought an exemption from the 
Securities and Exchange Commission for any aspect of the 
Matsushita tender offer (Exhibit F hereto). At around 6:00 
p.m., on December 13, 1990 the aforesaid Ostrager tele- 
phoned me to say that settlement papers were being drafted, 
that I could not have a copy of the drafts, but would receive a 
copy of the final version. Defendants have not responded to 
our request for documents concerning an SEC exemption. Fi- 
nally, an MCA press release issued December 12, 1990, states 
in part that: “The proposed [Delaware] settlement is condi- 
tioned upon dismissal of the remaining lawsuit [i.e., the in- 
stant one] in federal district court in Los Angeles.” That press 
release is Exhibit G hereto.
